An emotion snapshot is
\[
\mathcal E_\nu=
(\mathrm{emotion\_subject\_id},\mathrm{emotion\_definition\_id},
\mathrm{emotion\_version},
\mathrm{axis\_order},
y_\nu^{\rm hist},
\mathrm{snapshot\_ref},
t_\nu^E,\tau_{c,\nu},\mathrm{phase}_\nu).
\]
where
\[
y_\nu^{\rm hist}=Q_{\rm out}(Z_E^{\,\mathrm{read}}),\qquad
t_\nu^E=t_0+T_E\tau_{c,\nu}.
\]
emotion_subject_id explicitly identifies this modeled subject. Emotion expressed by or inferred about the user must be stored in separate observation fields, not substituted for this value. snapshot_ref uniquely identifies the state version / batch position read; it does not require transmission of the entire internal state. \(t_\nu^E\) is the time of the emotion value, not necessarily the historical occurrence time described by the memory content. Retain model version, quantization, and axis semantics together; an unlabeled row of numbers is insufficient.

At this stage, \(t_\nu^E\) may be represented exactly through the symbolic expressions above and a time-contract reference. Mark it as uninstantiated real-world time, not an actual clock reading. When real-world time is later connected, fill in known values and units under the same contract. A display string alone, time of unknown provenance, or a missing mapping does not establish restoration of the original time. [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5) and [16.4](#sec-16-4) still apply.

**Read timing.** For a normal new memory associated with an event, the default is phase=post_event: read \(Q_{\rm out}\) after the semantic commit of that event's emotion/sentiments/experience registration and before the next event changing relevant emotion states. This precedence is an explicit edge in the causal graph of [17.8](#sec-17-8). The pure read may occur after step 4 of [16.1](#sec-16-1). Reconstruct \(x\) from contemporaneous \(m,w,f\) and then quantize; do not reuse the previous output buffer or perform another emotion update. A batch-end, pre-event, or other defined snapshot requires its own phase and source version; these must not all be recorded as one undifferentiated \(t\).

When there is only a valid recording request and no emotion event, read the current state after both modules have advanced to the shared effective time, marking it state_at_capture. Recording times come from the content/memory contract and are locally finite. Tokens, message fragments, solver steps, and output refreshes must not automatically generate memories. If an interval memory needs multiple snapshots, use a finite set with individual time points and phases. Without a separate rule, an endpoint value must not masquerade as the interval average, nor is human retrospective assessment assumed to use time averaging.

**Recording operator.** Let \(\Pi_{\rm hist}\) be the historical-envelope projection supplied by the memory theory, with \(\mathscr A_M=\Pi_{\rm hist}(Z_M)\) its observable view; no particular physical database representation is required. For a valid envelope, define the historical view after addition or same-payload retransmission by
\[
\operatorname{put}(\mathscr A_M,\nu,\mathcal R_\nu)=
\begin{cases}
\mathscr A_M\cup\{\nu\mapsto\mathcal R_\nu\},&\nu\notin\operatorname{dom}\mathscr A_M,\\
\mathscr A_M,&\mathscr A_M(\nu)=\mathcal R_\nu.
\end{cases}
\]
Reject the same ID with a different payload under the existing contract. Memory's own \(\mathcal J_{\rm wrap}^M\) must be single-valued, preserve its admissible domain, and implement
\[
\Pi_{\rm hist}\!\left(\mathcal J_{\rm wrap}^M(Z_M;\mathcal R_\nu)\right)
=\operatorname{put}\!\left(\Pi_{\rm hist}(Z_M),\nu,\mathcal R_\nu\right).
\]
Its complete commit must also be atomically deduplicated by effect_id. Same-payload retransmission must be the identity on the complete state, not merely leave the historical view unchanged. The memory theory defines simultaneous effects on its other states; the projection does not imply that all are zero. Lift this memory-side operator to the joint commit \(\mathcal J_{\rm wrap}\), preserving the external emotion state and policy parameters. For emotion and policy parameters, require
\[
\pi_E\mathcal J_{\rm wrap}(Z_E,Z_M,\Theta_{\rm mem};\mathcal R_\nu)
=Z_E,\qquad
\pi_\Theta\mathcal J_{\rm wrap}(Z_E,Z_M,\Theta_{\rm mem};\mathcal R_\nu)
=\Theta_{\rm mem}.
\]
Envelope recording may change memory data, but must not additionally jump emotion states or train memory policies in the same step. Write completion and snapshot acknowledgements are not new stimuli, recall, or evidence. They neither replenish \(B_p\), add personality-experience fractions, nor incur another mental-load charge. Any genuine additional memory-side activity remains separately represented under the effort and event contracts.

The causal chain is explicitly
\[
\text{original event / read of existing memory}
\prec\text{emotion commit for that occurrence}
\prec\text{snapshot read}
\prec\text{memory-envelope commit}.
\]
The same effective \(t\) can accommodate this finite order without adding psychological time. Standard semantics treat snapshot reading and envelope commitment as ordered atomic operations within one recording effect: form a fixed payload, then store it on the memory side. Do not reread emotion at commit time and replace the captured value. If a later implementation separates these commits, include the pending payload, deduplication, and time versions in the specified interface state, and restate the augmented state and initial conditions under [17.9](#sec-17-9). Unaccounted hidden buffers are prohibited. An envelope being produced must not become an uncommitted input used to compute its own source emotion. Later retrieval or replay of a new envelope requires its own valid trigger and occurrence_id, still satisfying the causal and non-Zeno conditions of [17.8](#sec-17-8).

**Historical and current values.** \(y_\nu^{\rm hist}\) is the quantized reading output at capture time; storing it does not restore the complete \(e,m,w,b,s,f\). At capture, it was constrained by the then-current \(\mathcal C(m)\). It need not lie in the current \(\mathcal C(m)\), and historical values must not be reclipped to make them do so. Recall still reappraises historical content with the current state under [17.2](#sec-17-2); it does not set
\(X_E(t^+)=X_E(t_\nu^E)\) or directly use \(y_\nu^{\rm hist}\) as the current output.

For as long as a snapshot is retained as an original historical record, its values and definition version preserve their capture-time semantics. Retrospective feelings, reconstructed content, or revised interpretations formed by memory require fields and versions distinct from the original captured values. Verified transcription corrections also require provenance; current mood cannot fabricate the past. Rules for retaining or deleting original memories remain the memory theory's responsibility. Once data are gone, mark them unavailable instead of reconstructing history from current \(y\).

A normally completed new envelope requires a valid emotion snapshot and its time. Historical or input omissions may be explicitly marked incomplete/unavailable, but all-zero emotion cannot masquerade as compliance with the complete contract. This record format is an engineering data design; it does not claim that human memory stores eleven axes or exact times item by item.

If memory later uses historical emotion as input to thresholds, classifications, or continuous functions, explicitly state whether the object is the stored grid value or an inference about the unquantized state, together with endpoint, missing-value, and indeterminate-result semantics. Follow [17.14](#sec-17-14) and [Appendix F.3](#sec-f-3). Saving three decimal places does not make the value an error-free substitute for the internal value at that time.

<a id="sec-17-13"></a>

### 17.13 Shared Definitions and a Single Update Source

Emotion-data definitions and the time contract are already shared. Personality, goals, sentiments, or other constructs may be shared later, but this specification neither presumes that they must be shared nor precludes explicitly defined sharing.

Create a named, versioned description for each shareable definition:
\[
\mathfrak d=
(\mathrm{key},\mathrm{version},\mathcal D,
\mathrm{unit},\mathrm{meaning},
\mathrm{definition\_owner},\mathrm{state\_owner},
\mathrm{time\_role},\mathrm{readout},\mathrm{allowed\_uses}).
\]
\(\mathcal D\) is the value domain. time_role distinguishes current state, historical snapshot, parameter, and event total; readout specifies the source state. The definition owner is responsible for semantics and versions, and the state owner for evolution of the single live value. Another module may store the data. A common name or identical numbers do not establish equivalence of constructs.

The current emotion.output definition belongs to the emotion theory; its live value is given by \(Q_{\rm out}\), while memory stores time-tagged copies. Shared time belongs to the declared time contract. Internal emotion quantity \(b\) still represents only this model's affective-style baseline. Sharing the name “personality” does not make it a complete personality theory or another module's entire personality state.

Extension fields \(\mathcal X_\nu\) form a finite named map. Every value references a registered \(\mathfrak d\), read time/version, and availability. This specification defaults to \(\mathcal X_\nu=\varnothing\) and outputs no additional personality values or unquantized emotion axes. Unregistered constructs may remain uninterpreted data, but cannot enter the emotion equations. Extension fields must not bypass output-axis, quantization, or ownership limits. Enabling a field later requires an explicit mathematical effect and rechecking the complete interface conditions.

If a live state \(S\) is later jointly referenced by both theories, write the joint state as
\[
Z=(S,Z_E^{\rm private},Z_M^{\rm private}),
\]
with local views reading
\((S,Z_E^{\rm private})\) and \((S,Z_M^{\rm private})\), respectively. \(S\) has only one instance in the joint state, governed by the designated unique evolution \(F_S\) and event update \(\mathcal J_S\). Two independently learning copies of \(S_E,S_M\) are not synchronized merely by sharing a name. Multiple update sources, if necessary, require a separately specified single-valued composition rule, order, and domain-preservation conditions.

\(S\) is a reserved form here; this specification adds no live shared state. Existing historical emotion copies are not mutable aliases of current emotion. If affective-style data are shared later, memory may read/record them for defined purposes, while emotion-personality learning still updates only its own state and gains no authority to rewrite memory policies. Version changes to shared definitions also do not automatically reinterpret historical records. Necessary conversions must specify functions, applicability domains, information loss, and source versions.

<a id="sec-17-14"></a>

### 17.14 Usage Semantics and Information Bounds for Historical Quantized Values

This section defines how historical emotion readings are used; it enables no new memory encoding, classification, or retrieval rules. Let the grid spacing be \(q_y=10^{-3}\), and write
\[
\zeta_\nu=(x_J,\ldots,x_G,f)\big|_{\mathrm{snapshot}_\nu}
\]
for the eleven unquantized state readings at capture. This is analytical notation, not an additional external output; memory normally still stores \(y_\nu^{\rm hist}\). \(\zeta_\nu\) is not a calibrated true human subjective intensity either.

**Decisions about stored values versus inference about internal values.** If a future memory rule concerns the stored value itself, then, for example,
\[
C_{\theta,i}^{\rm stored}(y_\nu^{\rm hist})
=\mathbf1[y_{\nu i}^{\rm hist}\ge\theta],\qquad\theta\in[0,1],
\]
is already single-valued. A choice of \(>\) or another endpoint convention must be explicit in the rule version. Here \(\theta\) is only a placeholder for a future rule parameter; this specification sets no default threshold and enables no such classification. Compare stored integer grid values with explicitly defined numerical threshold semantics under [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5) and [16.3.1](#sec-16-3-1); an undefined epsilon must not alter equality.

This decision classifies only the stored value. It is not equivalent to
\(\mathbf1[\zeta_{\nu i}\ge\theta]\), nor does it prove that a human-like feeling crossed a psychological threshold at that time. An equality convention makes the result deterministic, but does not ensure that arbitrarily close emotion histories receive the same classification.

For an exact snapshot under the real-arithmetic specification, [15.2](#sec-15-2) gives
\[
|y_{\nu i}^{\rm hist}-\zeta_{\nu i}|<q_y\quad(i\in\mathcal I),
\qquad
|y_{\nu,\mathrm{Fat}}^{\rm hist}-\zeta_{\nu,\mathrm{Fat}}|\le q_y/2.
\]
Thus conservative closed-interval radii
\[
\varepsilon_i^Q=
\begin{cases}
q_y,&i\in\mathcal I,\\
q_y/2,&i=\mathrm{Fat}
\end{cases}
\]
yield
\[
\underline z_{\nu i}=\max\{0,y_{\nu i}^{\rm hist}-\varepsilon_i^Q\},\qquad
\overline z_{\nu i}=\min\{1,y_{\nu i}^{\rm hist}+\varepsilon_i^Q\},
\]
\[
\boxed{\zeta_\nu\in
\mathcal U_\nu^Q:=
\prod_{i\in\mathcal I\cup\{\mathrm{Fat}\}}
[\underline z_{\nu i},\overline z_{\nu i}].}
\]
The proof follows directly from the per-axis quantization bounds and \(\zeta_\nu\in[0,1]^{11}\). This box is only an outer information enclosure, not the exact preimage. It does not guarantee that every point in it produces the same \(y_\nu^{\rm hist}\), and it is not a psychological confidence interval. Unoutput historical \(m\) need not be obtained. Additional valid, traceable information may narrow the set, but current \(\mathcal C(m)\) must not be used to reclip past snapshots.

For a rule about the internal-value proposition \(\zeta_{\nu i}\ge\theta\), this information set defines the conservative truth value
\[
\mathsf T_{\theta,i}=
\begin{cases}
1,&\underline z_{\nu i}\ge\theta,\\
0,&\overline z_{\nu i}<\theta,\\
?,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
For the strict proposition \(\zeta_{\nu i}>\theta\), the corresponding conditions are
\(\underline z_{\nu i}>\theta\) and \(\overline z_{\nu i}\le\theta\).
The symbol \(?\) means that the available set is insufficient to decide; it is not an emotion value, probability, or new psychological state. A memory theory actually using this interface must specify how an indeterminate result enters its single-valued update. This specification does not default it to zero, rejection, or any behavioral command.

More generally, for a specified classification function \(C\), consider
\[
\mathcal L_\nu=\{C(z):z\in\mathcal U_\nu^Q\}.
\]
All possible values in the set have the same classification only if \(\mathcal L_\nu\) is a singleton. Multiple elements do not imply equal class probabilities. The set image under an arbitrary nonlinear \(C\) need not be easy to compute; this definition does not provide a solver for every classifier. A single-axis threshold is directly computable from the endpoint formulas above.

**Do not conflate numerical approximation with quantization error.** If the original snapshot is exactly quantized from an admissible stored state \(\widehat Z_E\) under [16.3.1](#sec-16-3-1), the pure quantization bound first applies relative to its reconstructed reading \(\widehat\zeta_\nu\). Only with an additional proved bound
\[
|\widehat\zeta_{\nu i}-\zeta_{\nu i}|\le\eta_{\nu i}
\]
under the same effective time, phase, event, and parameter contract may the preceding interval radius be replaced by
\(\varepsilon_i^Q+\eta_{\nu i}\) to bound the theoretical trajectory. With identical fixed \(\ell_i\), one may use
\[
\eta_{\nu i}:=
\ell_i E_{\nu i}^m+(1-\ell_i)E_{\nu i}^w
\]
as an effective state-error radius, where \(E^m,E^w\) are proved bounds on the two state errors. Fatigue uses its own error bound. If \(\ell\) also has representation differences or other errors, include those sources separately rather than omitting them through the fixed-parameter formula. Without a numerical-error bound, pure quantization bounds still describe only the state actually read; they do not become error bounds for the exact ODE trajectory. Unknown \(\eta\) must not be filled with zero. These are analytical quantities and provenance conditions, not additional emotion-output axes or reconstructed lost historical states.

**Quantization discontinuity and bounds that remain valid.** Fix an admissible \(m\). If an axis's constraint box contains adjacent grid points \(kq_y,(k+1)q_y\), take, near
\(x_*=(k+\tfrac12)q_y\),
\(x^\pm=x_*\pm\epsilon\), with \(\epsilon>0\) sufficiently small and both values inside the box. Inward clipping leaves their nearest grid points unchanged, so
\[
|y_i^+-y_i^-|=q_y,\qquad
|w_i^+-w_i^-|=\frac{2\epsilon}{1-\ell_i}.
\]
Hence the ratio
\(q_y(1-\ell_i)/(2\epsilon)\to\infty\). On an admissible state domain containing both sides of such a boundary, the capture map has no finite global Lipschitz constant. This is a proof along a sequence of admissible states; a single pair with a large finite difference quotient does not prove the result. A subdomain contained in one quantization cell may avoid this issue.

For any two admissible exact snapshots, the triangle inequality still gives
\[
|y_{\nu i}^{\rm hist}-y_{\mu i}^{\rm hist}|
\le|\zeta_{\nu i}-\zeta_{\mu i}|+2\varepsilon_i^Q.
\]
This additive error bound does not assert a Lipschitz quantization map; its right-hand side retains a constant term as the internal difference tends to zero. Explicit thresholds, information intervals, and this bound neither eliminate information lost to quantization nor automatically select classification behavior for the memory theory. For the same snapshot with version and phase, pure time-unit rescaling leaves \(y,\zeta,q_y\) unchanged, so the sets and decisions above remain identical. They are not defined by conversation turns or sampling-step counts.

<a id="sec-17-15"></a>

### 17.15 Conditions for Using Joint Guarantees

Assess existence and uniqueness under the complete assumptions of Section 17.9. Joint contraction, capture-map sensitivity, and the distinction between historical data and current feedback are addressed in [Appendix F.1](#sec-f-1), [Appendix F.2](#sec-f-2), and [Appendix F.3](#sec-f-3), respectively. A coupled implementation must satisfy all cited applicability conditions.

<a id="sec-a"></a>

## Appendix A Solution Conditions and Invariant Domains

<a id="sec-a-1"></a>

### A.1 Conditions and Solution Concept

Fix finite sets of objects and simultaneously effective sources. Events, modes, sources, and override acceptance, expiry, revocation, and invalidation switches must be locally finite on every finite abstract-time interval; Zeno behavior is prohibited. Timing of effort-override activation, expiry, revocation, and loss of eligibility is part of the valid external input. Other specified budget-exhaustion cases follow the contract below. Arbitrarily added state guards and switching rules are not automatically covered by this proposition. Continuous inputs and \(j_E\) are bounded and measurable. If determined by internal state, they must be locally Lipschitz within each fixed mode and solved simultaneously through the same equations. Masks, cause scopes, and semantic ordering are explicit discrete data; arbitrary classifiers are not assumed Lipschitz.

Execute events by \((\tau,\mathrm{order})\). Each appraisal-inference rule must be continuous and locally Lipschitz on the effective state domain, or its encoded result must be supplied as a fixed external input. Cut off sources at memory-budget exhaustion; finite accounts and locally finite allocations must not produce infinitely many exhaustion switches.

Each fixed-mode interval has a unique Carathéodory solution. Event common flows, reappraisal flows, sentiments maps, and experience-registration maps are unique. Concatenating them in the specified timing yields a unique hybrid trajectory.

Reason: the positive-part operator, clip, and finite products are Lipschitz on bounded domains; the denominators \(1-\ell_{\max},v_{\max},H_b,\delta_E,\delta_r,Q_{\max},1+\rho_ff,1+W_o\) have positive lower bounds. The \(b\) equation uses the nonsingular \(P^E,Q_E\) form, not \(P^E/Q_E\) to evaluate the vector field at zero. Finite event doses give the logarithm's argument a positive lower bound. Common and reappraisal flows are Lipschitz on compact domains. Compensation \(s_f^\chi=\exp(\chi\log s_f)\) is smooth with bounded derivatives on \(1\le s_f\le1+\rho_f\), \(0\le\chi\le1\), and \(\delta_r+C_fS_r\ge\delta_r>0\). Fatigue load and compensatory effort are evaluated explicitly from the same current state, without an algebraic loop. State-provided \(q_{\rm rest},g_E,\chi\) must satisfy the same Lipschitz conditions. Experience participation masks are piecewise-constant activity data; \((D^{\rm exp},I^{\rm exp})\) are bounded integrals. Ratios are submitted only for positive participation duration; zero duration uses the explicit discrete branch. No uniform ratio-sensitivity bound is claimed over arbitrary collections of experiences whose durations approach zero. The invariant domain below permits continuation to any finite time.

<a id="sec-a-2"></a>

### A.2 Psychological Domains, Experience Capacity, and Counters

Let \(k_E=a_E^c+\lambda_D+j_E/Q_{\max}\ge0\). At \(Q_E=0\), \(\dot Q_E=j_E\ge0\); at \(Q_E=Q_{\max}\),
\(\dot Q_E=-(a_E^c+\lambda_D)Q_{\max}\le0\).
Also,
\[
\dot P_i^E=j_Ez_i^E-k_EP_i^E,\qquad
\frac d{d\tau}(Q_E-P_i^E)=j_E(1-z_i^E)-k_E(Q_E-P_i^E).
\]
Hence \(0\le P_i^E\le Q_E\le Q_{\max}\) is invariant. The registration map is a nonnegative mixture with the same coefficients and preserves these bounds. The discarded increment satisfies
\(c_l-(Q_{\max}-Q_E^-)(1-e^{-c_l/Q_{\max}})\ge0\), since \(1-e^{-x}\le x\).

\(b\) evolves toward a convex target in \(\mathcal B\); \(m\) evolves toward a target in \([0,1]\). At \(e=0\), both down-regulation positive parts vanish and the base derivative is nonnegative. At \(e=1\), neither the base nor down-regulation term points outward. \(w,s\) likewise point inward at both endpoints. At \(f=0\), \(\dot f=a_f\mathcal L\ge0\); at \(f=1\), \(\dot f=-b_f\mathcal R_f\le0\). The fatigue domain is therefore invariant even when compensatory effort depends on \(f\). Event flows also do not point outward at the endpoints of \(e,w,s\); other psychological states do not jump.

All psychological states and the current L1 summary are therefore bounded. \(\Xi_E,D_E\) and the number of archived evidence records may grow with unlimited use, but need not be added to the current ODE as a per-record queue. Finite inputs and non-Zeno behavior keep counters finite over finite time. A fixed-size summary does not mean a permanently fixed-size database.

<a id="sec-a-3"></a>

### A.3 Moving Constraint Box

From \(x_i=\ell_i m_i+(1-\ell_i)w_i,\ w_i\in[0,1]\),
\[
L_i(m)\le x_i\le U_i(m).
\]
Also, \(\dot L_i=\dot U_i=\ell_i\dot m_i\). At the lower boundary,
\(\dot x_i-\dot L_i=\lambda_{x,i}(P_i-L_i)\ge0\),
and at the upper boundary,
\(\dot x_i-\dot U_i=\lambda_{x,i}(P_i-U_i)\le0\).
In every event flow, \(m\) is fixed and \(w\) remains in its domain, so sequential composition of simultaneous events also preserves the constraints. Personality summaries and attention mechanisms change subsequent rates without violating this geometric relationship. Three-decimal output still uses the inward grid mapping of [Section 15](#sec-15).

<a id="sec-a-4"></a>

### A.4 Claims Not Covered

These proofs do not imply uniqueness of arbitrary external feedback loops, absence of multistability, convergence for all inputs, unique identification of personality from emotion outputs, or human validity of the equations. [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6) provides an unstable equilibrium within admissible parameters, distinguishing bounded values from equilibrium stability. Adding fatigue observation through eleven-axis output does not itself prove complete-state or parameter identifiability. Joint guarantees with memory in shared time follow [Sections 17.9–17.10](#sec-17-9) and [Appendix F.1](#sec-f-1); the single-module proof here does not automatically extend to arbitrary intermodule feedback loops.

<a id="sec-b"></a>

## Appendix B Long-Term Properties and Adaptation Bounds

<a id="sec-b-1"></a>

### B.1 Recovery with Fixed Baseline and No Sources

Let \(b=b_*\) be fixed, with no new events or continuous emotional sources, so \(T=m\). The two regulation terms then combine as
\(-(K_i+D_i^{\rm att})[e_i-m_i]_+\), with nonnegative bounded coefficient. Let
\(E_i=e_i-b_{*,i},M_i=m_i-b_{*,i}\), and choose any \(\kappa<a<1\):
\[
\mathcal V_i=\max(|E_i|,|M_i|/a).
\]
When the first term dominates, \(|M_i|\le a|E_i|\). If \(E_i\ge0\), the additional positive part only increases the inward effect; if \(E_i<0\), then \(E_i<M_i\), so the positive part vanishes. Hence
\[
D^+|E_i|\le-\lambda_{e,i}(1-a)\mathcal V_i.
\]
When the second term dominates,
\[
D^+(|M_i|/a)\le-\lambda_{m,i}(1-\kappa/a)\mathcal V_i.
\]
Thus
\[
D^+\mathcal V_i\le-c_i\mathcal V_i,\quad
c_i=\min\{\lambda_{e,i}(1-a),\lambda_{m,i}(1-\kappa/a)\}>0.
\]
This estimate remains valid when regulation coefficients depend on fatigue and state. Lipschitz continuity of \(F\) and stable filtering give \(e,m,w,x\to b_*\). If additionally \(\mathcal L\to0\) and eventually recovery opportunity satisfies \(\mathcal R_f\ge r_0>0\), then \(f\to0\). Load tending to zero alone does not cover the case of zero recovery opportunity. Sentiments need not be cleared.

<a id="sec-b-2"></a>

### B.2 Personality Drift and Effective Experience Amount

\[
|b_i-b_{0,i}|\le\rho_b,\qquad
|\dot b_i|\le(\rho_b/H_b)r_E\le\rho_b\mu_E/H_b.
\]
If all integrated profiles form the same fixed target \(t_i\),
\[
b_i(\tau)=t_i+[b_i(0)-t_i]
e^{-[\Xi_E(\tau)-\Xi_E(0)]/H_b}.
\]
Thus \(H_b\) still denotes an effective experience amount, not months.

If the initial pending amount and all subsequent allocations have finite total, the accounting equation in [Section 10.3](#sec-10-3) gives
\[
\int_0^\infty r_E\,d\tau
\le Q_E(0)+\sum_l c_l+\int_0^\infty j_E\,d\tau<\infty.
\]
Therefore \(b\) has finite total variation and converges to \(b_\infty\). When integration is suspended, \(\dot b=0\); fading of unintegrated data does not directly reset personality.

<a id="sec-b-3"></a>

### B.3 Recency and Frequency Interpretation of the Statistics

Separate the contribution of unintegrated data preceding time \(T\) as \(Q_{\rm old},P_{\rm old}\). Subsequent continuous evolution multiplies it by
\(\exp[-\int_T^\tau(a_E^c+\lambda_D+j_E/Q_{\max})du]\),
and every new registration further multiplies it by \(e^{-c_l/Q_{\max}}\). Therefore
\[
0\le Q_{\rm old}(\tau)\le Q_{\max}e^{-\lambda_D(\tau-T)},\quad
0\le P_{{\rm old},i}(\tau)\le Q_{\max}e^{-\lambda_D(\tau-T)}.
\]
This bounds the **direct absolute contribution of the unintegrated summary**, traced from its original allocation. It does not assert a necessarily decreasing proportion: without new data, that proportion may remain one. Nor does it erase the learned \(b\). Indirect effects of old experience transmitted through \(b,m\) and later new profiles are not covered by this single exponential bound. Capacity and discarding prevent unbounded pending amounts and indefinite queues of old data, but do not guarantee immediate reversal of personality after every environmental change or completed adaptation within a real-world quarter.

To analyze frequency, fix a continuous effective registration rate \(j_E=j>0\), fixed \(u_E=u>0\), and a fixed complete-axis mean profile \(\bar z\), temporarily treated as an externally encoded input. Then
\[
\dot Q_E=j(1-Q_E/Q_{\max})-\lambda_DQ_E
-\frac{\mu_Eu Q_E}{\delta_E+Q_E}.
\]
The right-hand side is strictly decreasing on \([0,Q_{\max}]\) with opposite signs at the endpoints, giving a unique stable \(Q_E^*\in(0,Q_{\max})\). Since
\[
\frac d{d\tau}(P_i^E-\bar z_iQ_E)
=-k_E(P_i^E-\bar z_iQ_E),
\],
we have \(P_i^E/Q_E\to\bar z_i\), and therefore
\[
b_i\to (1-\rho_b)b_{0,i}+\rho_b\bar z_i.
\]
If complete experience type \(h\) has effective proportion \(\pi_h\), then \(\bar z_i=\sum_h\pi_h z_{hi}\), including types with low values on that axis. This is not merely the conditional average over cases where that axis was selected.

This result assumes a fixed profile and continuous registration. In the actual model, profiles are generated from \(e\), affected by residual responses and sampling, and event rates may be nonstationary. Arbitrary discrete histories therefore need not converge exactly to objective event frequency times intensity.

<a id="sec-b-4"></a>

### B.4 Conditional Equilibria Under Fixed Ongoing Conditions

With no events, fixed \(b,\alpha,A\), and no deliberate regulation \(K=0\),
\[
e_i^*=\frac{(1-\alpha_i)(1-\kappa)b_i+\alpha_iA_i}
{1-\kappa(1-\alpha_i)},\quad m_i^*=(1-\kappa)b_i+\kappa e_i^*,
\quad w_i^*=F_i(e^*,m^*),\ x_i^*=P_i(e^*,m^*).
\]
At equilibrium, \(e^*=T^*\), so the attentional-disengagement term vanishes and creates no additional fixed gain cap. If additionally \(D^{\rm att}=0\), the linear \(e,m\) subsystem has negative trace and positive determinant and is stable. General closed-loop stability does not follow automatically from this linear special case.

For fixed nonnegative \(K,D^{\rm att}\), when \(A>b\) the positive part is active and \(e^*\le T^*\); the attentional-disengagement term still vanishes:
\[
e^*=\frac{\lambda_e\alpha A+
(1-\kappa)[\lambda_e(1-\alpha)+K]b}
{\lambda_e[1-\kappa(1-\alpha)]+(1-\kappa)K}.
\]
When \(A=b\), \(e^*=b\). When \(A<b\), the deliberate down-regulation positive part is inactive, recovering the formula for \(K=0\). The default \(K\) is coupled to \(f,e,m\) and must be solved together with the policy, not replaced by an assumed constant.

\(\alpha=1,K=0\) permits \(e^*=A\). \(x^*=\operatorname{clip}_{\mathcal C(m^*)}(A)\) remains subject to the required long-term spatial constraint; there is no fixed 2/3 cap.

<a id="sec-b-5"></a>

### B.5 Recovery Under Finite Driving

Assume that after some time there are no new external emotion events and nonreplay continuous emotional sources stop. Effective replay satisfies
\[
\sum_k\xi_k^{\rm rep}<\infty,\qquad
\int_0^\infty\sum_{j\in\rm rep}v_j\,d\tau<\infty.
\]
Also assume \(b\) is fixed or has finite total variation under [Appendix B.2](#sec-b-2). Let \(E=e-b(\tau),M=m-b(\tau)\). Relative to the source-free \(-(K+D^{\rm att})[e-m]_+\),
\[
|[e-T]_+-[e-m]_+|\le|T-m|\le\alpha.
\]
so
\[
D^+\mathcal V_i\le-c_i\mathcal V_i+
(\lambda_{e,i}+\eta_i^{\rm att})\alpha_i+
|\dot b_i|/a.
\]
[Section 11.5](#sec-11-5) gives event bounds \(|\Delta e_i|\le\gamma_e\xi_k\) and
\(|\Delta w_i|\le C_w\xi_k\); \(m,b\) does not jump.
The disturbance integral and total jump magnitude are finite, and the exponential-convolution tail tends to zero. Hence \(E,M\to0\); filtering by \(w\) and summable jumps then give
\[
e,m,w,x\to b_\infty.
\]
Fatigue is assessed separately using the load and cumulative-recovery conditions of [Section 9.3.3](#sec-9-3-3); emotion convergence does not automatically imply \(f\to0\). A finite total memory budget can guarantee the replay condition, but unresolved concerns, unlimited new data, and sustained external driving lie outside this proposition.

<a id="sec-b-6"></a>

### B.6 Time Averages and Limits of the Claims

For fixed \(b\), when time averages exist,
\[
\overline m=(1-\kappa)b+\kappa\overline e,\qquad
\overline m-b=\frac{\kappa}{1-\kappa}\overline{(e-m)}.
\]
These results do not prove that humans eventually recover, that arbitrary emotion-dependent schedules lack multistability, or that outputs have a calibrated correspondence to human subjective severity.

<a id="sec-b-7"></a>

### B.7 Boundary Between the Fatigue Subsystem and Emotional Learning

The integral formula in [Section 9.3.3](#sec-9-3-3) follows along the solution from the integrating factor of a first-order linear equation with variable coefficients. For fixed external actual load, when \(k_f>0\) the error satisfies \(\dot{(f-f^*)}=-k_f(f-f^*)\), so the scalar equilibrium is exponentially stable. When \(k_f=0\), every \(f\) is an equilibrium.

For general coupled inputs, if \(A_f\to0\) and eventually \(B_f\ge b_f r_0>0\), then
\[
\dot f\le A_f-b_f r_0 f
\]
and an exponential-convolution tail bound give \(f\to0\). If only \(A_f=0\), the integral representation directly implies that, for initial \(f>0\), recovery to zero occurs only when \(\int \mathcal R_f=\infty\). These are mathematical conditions, not a guarantee that every human recovers by resting.

Automatic compensation makes load depend on \(f,e,m\). This specification proves boundedness, well-posedness, and the stated conditional properties; it does not claim that the complete feedback loop has only one fatigue equilibrium. \(g_E(u_{\rm task}+u_{\rm rec})\) contains no regulation effort, so the personality-rate bound in [Appendix B.2](#sec-b-2) applies without a direct negative-valence acceleration pathway. Fatigue changing experience profiles through actual emotion histories remains emotional learning, not memory evolution. [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2) gives an additional uniqueness result for a class with fixed regulation demand; [Appendix D.4](#sec-d-4) gives its necessary conditions for closed-loop equilibria. Neither extends to complete closed-loop uniqueness. This does not prove complete causal independence of fatigue and personality; it limits the states and direct rate sources this module may change.

<a id="sec-b-8"></a>

### B.8 Personality-Adaptation Magnitude, Effective Experience, and Output Visibility

This section distinguishes internal evolution, changes in resting equilibria, and quantized readings, correcting the inference that learning stops whenever changes fall below the grid spacing.

Let \(\tau_1\ge\tau_0\),
\[
E_b(\tau_1,\tau_0)
=\frac{\Xi_E(\tau_1)-\Xi_E(\tau_0)}{H_b}
=\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}\frac{r_E(u)}{H_b}\,du.
\]
\(E_b\) is dimensionless cumulative effective experience, not years. Along the actual solution,
\[
b_i(\tau_1)=e^{-E_b(\tau_1,\tau_0)}b_i(\tau_0)
+\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}
e^{-E_b(\tau_1,u)}\frac{r_E(u)}{H_b}t_i^E(u)\,du.
\]
At \(Q_E>0\), \(t_i^E=(1-\rho_b)b_{0,i}+\rho_bP_i^E/Q_E\); at \(Q_E=0\), \(r_E=0\), and any admissible \(t^E\) may be used in this integral representation without changing the result. The continuous vector field still uses the nonsingular formula in [Section 10.3](#sec-10-3).

Let \(\zeta_i^E=P_i^E/Q_E\) be the profile in the pool, with any admissible value at zero amount. It differs from the newly registered input \(z_i^E\) in [Section 10.3](#sec-10-3). For \(E_b>0\), define the actual integration-weighted profile
\[
\widehat z_i=
\frac{\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}
e^{-E_b(\tau_1,u)}(r_E(u)/H_b)\zeta_i^E(u)\,du}
{1-e^{-E_b(\tau_1,\tau_0)}}\in[0,1].
\]
The integral weights sum to \(1-e^{-E_b}\), so if \(b_i(\tau_0)=b_{0,i}\),
\[
\boxed{b_i(\tau_1)-b_{0,i}
=\rho_b(1-e^{-E_b})(\widehat z_i-b_{0,i}).}
\]
Hence
\[
|b_i(\tau_1)-b_{0,i}|
\le\rho_b(1-e^{-E_b})
\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{u:r_E(u)>0}|\zeta_i^E(u)-b_{0,i}|.
\]
For zero effective integration amount, directly use \(b_i(\tau_1)=b_i(\tau_0)\). When the initial value differs from the anchor, use the complete integral formula above; the initial difference must not be omitted.

**Magnitude differs from speed.** With the encoded profile and integration history fixed, reducing \(H_b\) changes \(1-e^{-E_b}\), but cannot move the convex target outside its range. \(\rho_b\) controls learnable magnitude; the profile controls direction and content. In the complete feedback loop, \(b\) can in turn affect the profile. This conditional comparison is not global monotonicity across all environments.

For a fixed target throughout and an initial value different from that target, the fraction of the target difference completed is \(1-e^{-\Delta\Xi_E/H_b}\). Thus fraction \(p\in(0,1)\) corresponds to
\[
\Delta\Xi_E=-H_b\log(1-p).
\]
This is an experience-amount relation, not a basis for specifying events per year or real-world speed. Any revision of \(H_b\) must still satisfy the timescale relations in [Section 12.2](#sec-12-2). More precisely, if \(\zeta_i^E(u)=\bar z_i\) wherever integration weight is nonzero, initially \(b_i(\tau_0)=b_{0,i}\), and
\(E_\infty=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}E_b(\tau,\tau_0)\in[0,\infty]\), then
\[
b_i(\infty)-b_{0,i}
=\rho_b(1-e^{-E_\infty})(\bar z_i-b_{0,i}),
\qquad e^{-\infty}:=0.
\]
Only when \(E_\infty=\infty\) may the weight generally be replaced by 1 to obtain the full target difference \(\rho_b(\bar z_i-b_{0,i})\); finite experience retains an initial-value weight. If the target already equals the initial value, the difference is zero, and no completion percentage for a nonzero difference is defined. Arbitrary nonstationary profiles do not automatically have this fixed-target limit. This is a limit of \(b\); return of the other emotion states to that baseline still requires the corresponding conditions of [Appendix B.1](#sec-b-1) or [Appendix B.5](#sec-b-5).

**Quantization visibility.** For any fixed admissible \(b_*\), the source-free resting equilibrium with fixed baseline satisfies \(e=m=w=x=b_*\). Define
\[
Y_i^{\rm eq}(b_*)=\text{quantization in Section 15.2 at }x=m=b_*\text{},\qquad q_y=0.001.
\]
For two such equilibria \(b^{(1)},b^{(2)}\),
\[
\boxed{\left|
[Y_i^{\rm eq}(b^{(2)})-Y_i^{\rm eq}(b^{(1)})]
-[b_i^{(2)}-b_i^{(1)}]\right|<2q_y.}
\]
This follows from two inward-quantization errors each smaller than \(q_y\) and the triangle inequality. \(\Delta y^{\rm rest}\) must not be treated as exactly equal to \(\rho_b\Delta z\).

An internal change smaller than one grid spacing may cross a quantization boundary or leave the reading unchanged; internal accumulation continues in full. The bound above gives a sufficient condition, \(|\Delta b_i|\ge2q_y\), for different equilibrium readings. It is neither a human perceptual threshold nor a required model-change magnitude. \(Y^{\rm eq}\) is the reading of an equilibrium state. If a trajectory converges exactly to a quantization boundary, this does not establish a unique limit of the quantized sequence.

**Tighter bounds away from constraint-box boundaries.** If both equilibria satisfy
\[
(1-\ell_i)b_i^{(j)}\ge q_y/2,\qquad
(1-\ell_i)(1-b_i^{(j)})\ge q_y/2,\quad j=1,2,
\],
their respective nearest grid points already lie inside the box, so inward clipping does not move them. Each pointwise error is then at most \(q_y/2\), yielding
\[
\boxed{\left|
[Y_i^{\rm eq}(b^{(2)})-Y_i^{\rm eq}(b^{(1)})]
-[b_i^{(2)}-b_i^{(1)}]\right|\le q_y.}
\]
The two distances in the condition are \(b-L_i\) and \(U_i-b\), not requirements on personality-change magnitude. This tighter bound does not apply near endpoints; in particular, the minimum-nonzero-reading effect in [Appendix B.13](#sec-b-13) remains.

**Integration weight is not always completion.** For a general time-varying target, \(e^{-E_b}\) is the retained weight of the initial state, and \(1-e^{-E_b}\) the total weight of subsequent integration targets. They do not mean that the same percentage of progress toward a fixed personality target has been completed. That interpretation is valid only under the fixed-target conditions above. Changing \(H_b\) may still matter over finite-experience phases. A history already close to its fixed target does not establish that changing speed has no effect in general.

**Error bound for an approximately fixed target.** Choose a reference target \(\bar t_i\) and suppose that wherever integration weight is nonzero,
\[
|t_i^E(u)-\bar t_i|\le\epsilon_i,\qquad\epsilon_i\ge0,
\]
Let
\[
b_i^{\rm ref}(\tau_1)
=e^{-E_b}b_i(\tau_0)+(1-e^{-E_b})\bar t_i.
\]
The same integral representation and nonnegative weights give
\[
\boxed{|b_i(\tau_1)-b_i^{\rm ref}(\tau_1)|
\le(1-e^{-E_b})\epsilon_i.}
\]
If \(\bar t_i\ne b_i(\tau_0)\), then
\[
\left|
\frac{b_i(\tau_1)-b_i(\tau_0)}
{\bar t_i-b_i(\tau_0)}
-(1-e^{-E_b})
\right|
\le
\frac{(1-e^{-E_b})\epsilon_i}
{|\bar t_i-b_i(\tau_0)|}.
\]
This supplies an explicit error for treating the weight as an approximate completion fraction. Relative error may be large when the reference difference is near zero. Similar weighted profiles \(\widehat z\) at a few times do not guarantee that the entire pool-target history \(t^E\) satisfies the same \(\epsilon_i\). Bounding along the actual solution does not require assuming that a profile affected in turn by \(H_b\) is an independent input.

With baseline adaptation alone, some input histories with low average responses may yield very small output differences. An average from a particular history is not a global upper bound and does not enable learning of \(g_i\).

<a id="sec-b-9"></a>

### B.9 Effective Experience Rate and a Necessary Lower Bound on \(H_b\)

From the engineering timescale-separation condition in [Section 12.2](#sec-12-2), let
\[
r_{\min}=\min_i r_{m,i}>0,
\]
Then
\[
\boxed{\frac{\mu_E}{H_b}\le c_Er_{\min}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
H_b\ge\frac{\mu_E}{c_Er_{\min}}.}
\]
This is a requirement of the engineering parameter domain, not a personality time constant supplied by psychology. It restricts permissible \(H_b\) and cannot be ignored merely to accelerate integration. \(c_E\le.2\) and default \(\rho_b=.1\) are engineering choices, not calibrated human constants. Any later evidence-driven modification requires formal revision together with the proof and parameter domain.

From \(0\le Q_E\le Q_{\max}\) and \(0\le u_E\le1\), a capacity bound tighter than \(r_E\le\mu_E\) is also available:
\[
r_E=\mu_Eu_E\frac{Q_E}{\delta_E+Q_E}
\le\mu_Eu_E\frac{Q_{\max}}{\delta_E+Q_{\max}},
\]
\[
\boxed{\frac{dE_b(\tau,\tau_0)}{d\tau}
\le\frac{\mu_E}{H_b}
\frac{Q_{\max}}{\delta_E+Q_{\max}}u_E(\tau)
\le c_Er_{\min}
\frac{Q_{\max}}{\delta_E+Q_{\max}}u_E(\tau).}
\]
Integration gives:
\[
E_b(\tau_1,\tau_0)\le
\frac{\mu_E}{H_b}\frac{Q_{\max}}{\delta_E+Q_{\max}}
\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}u_E(\tau)\,d\tau
\le c_Er_{\min}(\tau_1-\tau_0).
\]
The final bound is looser; the preceding expression shows that no progress occurs without actual integration activity. The capacity bound is an upper bound, not a guarantee of a permanently full pool. Insufficient registration, summary discarding, and intermittent activity may further reduce actual integration.

Only for a fixed target different from the initial value, and a required fraction \(p\in(0,1)\), may the necessary effective experience from [Appendix B.8](#sec-b-8) be substituted to obtain the necessary abstract duration
\[
\Delta\tau\ge
\frac{H_b}{\mu_E}\frac{\delta_E+Q_{\max}}{Q_{\max}}
[-\log(1-p)].
\]
This is a conditional lower bound on even the fastest possible completion, not a sufficient condition for attaining the fraction or a prediction in real-world years.

Using the engineering initial values in [Section 12.3](#sec-12-3) purely for parameter calculation:
\[
r_{\min}=\frac{1.005-\sqrt{1.000025}}2
\approx0.002493750039,
\qquad
\frac{\mu_E}{c_Er_{\min}}\approx80.200499997.
\]
Thus, with the other relevant defaults fixed, setting \(H_b\) to 80 cannot be claimed to satisfy the bound. Use the exact formula; the display “approximately 80” does not define an admissible lower limit. Current \(H_b=100\) remains admissible. With current \(Q_{\max}=8,\delta_E=1\), the capacity bound on the maximum effective-experience growth rate is
\[
\dot E_b\le(\mu_E/H_b)(8/9)u_E
\approx0.000355555556\,u_E.
\]
These are deductions from the specification's engineering parameters, with no additional human constants or real-clock connection.

Under psychological-time rescaling, \(\mu_E,r_{\min}\) are both divided by the scale factor, so the lower bound on \(H_b\) is unchanged. Under an experience-unit change, \(\mu_E,H_b\) scale together, leaving capacity ratios and \(E_b\) unchanged. These bounds agree with the time semantics of [Section 14](#sec-14).

<a id="sec-b-10"></a>

### B.10 Personality-Change Scales and Percentages

“A certain effect within a year” involves at least time, experience content, a reference value, and a response scale; a percentage alone is insufficient. For two abstract times of the same individual, define the internal baseline difference
\[
\Delta b_i=b_i(\tau_1)-b_i(\tau_0).
\]
Since \(b_i\in\mathcal B_i\) and the interval width is \(\rho_b\), any two times satisfy
\[
|\Delta b_i|\le\rho_b.
\]
Thus engineering default \(\rho_b=.1\) cannot produce a single-axis baseline difference of \(0.18\) full-scale units. This is not equivalent to “personality changes by only 10%,” because \(b\) is not a complete personality scale.

The following quantities are not interchangeable:

| Quantity | Definition and limits |
|---|---|
| Full-scale difference | \(\Delta b_i\), because the scale spans 0–1; \(0.18\) is an 18-percentage-point difference on that scale, not 18% of all personality |
| Change relative to the original value | \(\Delta b_i/\lvert b_i(\tau_0)\rvert\), defined only for a nonzero original value. It does not apply to the default zero anchor. Near zero, this ratio may be large despite a tiny absolute difference |
| Fixed-target completion fraction | \(p_i=(b_i(\tau_1)-b_i(\tau_0))/(t_i-b_i(\tau_0))=1-e^{-E_b}\), only for a target fixed throughout and different from the initial value |
| Integration weight for a general history | \(W_b=1-e^{-E_b}\), neither a completion fraction under time-varying targets nor an amount of behavioral change |
| Relative difference between two engineering configurations | For example, \((\Delta b_i^{(2)}-\Delta b_i^{(1)})/\lvert\Delta b_i^{(1)}\rvert\), with nonzero denominator. It compares two model outcomes, not one person's annual change |
| Standardized human effect size | If a study's \(d=\Delta\overline v/s_{\rm ref}\) is used, its denominator is the standard deviation specified by that definition. \(d=.18\) does not mean an 18% change and does not directly correspond to \(b_i=.18\) here |
| Difference in emotional expression | Separate differences in raw emotion trajectories, baselines, and final quantized readings, related through the state equations and output map. \(\Delta b\) cannot directly be treated as \(\Delta y\) |

If a human scale permits positive affine recoding \(v'=av+c\) (\(a>0\)), then
\[
\frac{\Delta v'}{v'_0}=\frac{a\Delta v}{av_0+c}
\]
changes with origin \(c\). When the origin has no substantive meaning of zero personality, a percentage relative to the original value is not scale-invariant. This is a limitation of scale arithmetic, not a claim that all human scales are identical.

A measurement mapping from human scale scores \(v\) to model states has not been established. Big Five emotional stability, neuroticism, average daily states, and the ten-axis baseline must not be treated as one construct. \(b\) is a limited affective-style proxy, not a complete representation of personality, emotional reactivity, or regulation ability.

**Pure speed comparison with identical profiles and effective experience.** If \(H_2<H_1\), two processes integrate from the same initial value toward the same fixed target with nonzero difference, and both have consumed effective experience \(C>0\), then the change-magnitude ratio
\[
R_H(C)=\frac{1-e^{-C/H_2}}{1-e^{-C/H_1}}
\]
satisfies
\[
1<R_H(C)<H_1/H_2,\quad
\lim_{C\downarrow0}R_H=H_1/H_2,\quad
\lim_{C\to\infty}R_H=1.
\]
Proof: \(h(z)=1-e^{-z}\) is strictly increasing and strictly concave, with \(h(0)=0\), so \(h(kz)>h(z)\) and \(h(kz)<kh(z)\) (\(k>1,z>0\)). The two limits follow from first-order expansion and exponential limits. This is a conditional comparison. If profiles or engagement also change with parameters in the complete feedback loop, it cannot be directly transferred to a ratio bound for the whole model.

<a id="sec-b-11"></a>

### B.11 Real-World Time Interface and Reachability Conditions

For any \(\Delta\tau=\tau_1-\tau_0>0\), define
\[
\mathcal A_E(\tau_1,\tau_0)
=\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}
u_E(\tau)\frac{Q_E(\tau)}{\delta_E+Q_E(\tau)}\,d\tau,
\]
\[
\overline a_E=\mathcal A_E/\Delta\tau,\qquad
0\le\overline a_E\le q_{\rm cap}
:=\frac{Q_{\max}}{\delta_E+Q_{\max}}<1.
\]
This is an average fraction jointly determined by actual integration activity and available summaries, not event frequency, user-online proportion, or a new psychological state. Exactly,
\[
\boxed{E_b=(\mu_E/H_b)\mathcal A_E
=(\mu_E/H_b)\overline a_E\Delta\tau.}
\]

Retain uncalibrated \(T_{\rm ref}>0\). Only after a real-world interval length \(D_{\rm real}\) and time mapping are specified can one write
\[
\Delta\tau=D_{\rm real}/T_{\rm ref},\qquad
E_b=\frac{\mu_E}{H_bT_{\rm ref}}\overline a_E D_{\rm real}.
\]
No actual clock values are substituted. “One to two months” and “one year” may denote future interval lengths, but are not natural time constants directly set by this specification. Changing \(T_{\rm ref}\) converts the real-world timing of emotion, fatigue, and personality together; personality cannot alone be relabeled as operating over months while other conversions remain fixed. Under [Section 14](#sec-14), \(\mathcal A_E\) and \(\Delta\tau\) are multiplied by the scale factor, \(\overline a_E\) is unchanged, and \(\mu_E,T_{\rm ref}\) are divided by that factor, preserving the same process.

For a fixed target different from the initial value, suppose independent evidence specifies a desired completion range
\(0<p_{\rm lo}\le p_{\rm hi}<1\). Let
\(c_{\rm lo}=-\log(1-p_{\rm lo})\) and \(c_{\rm hi}=-\log(1-p_{\rm hi})\).
For fixed known \(\mathcal A_E>0\), compatibility requires
\[
c_{\rm lo}\le(\mu_E/H_b)\mathcal A_E\le c_{\rm hi},
\],
equivalently
\[
\boxed{
H_b\in
\left[\frac{\mu_E\mathcal A_E}{c_{\rm hi}},
      \frac{\mu_E\mathcal A_E}{c_{\rm lo}}\right]
\cap
\left[\frac{\mu_E}{c_Er_{\min}},\infty\right).
}
\]
An empty intersection means that the requested range, known activity history, and timescale-separation conditions are incompatible. With activity and registration amounts fixed, [B.11.1](#sec-b-11-1) proves that \(\mathcal A_E\) is indeed independent of \(H_b\), so this explicit interval applies directly. Only when relevant inputs or other compared quantities change with \(H_b\) is the implicit set needed:
\[
\{H_b>0:
c_{\rm lo}\le(\mu_E/H_b)\mathcal A_E[H_b]\le c_{\rm hi},\
\mu_E/H_b\le c_Er_{\min}\},
\]
Fixing an old activity amount does not produce an exact inversion of the complete feedback loop. This is an admissible parameter set, not a calibration procedure; the specification assigns no human \(p_{\rm lo},p_{\rm hi}\).

For any admissible activity, completing at least \(p_{\rm lo}\) within the interval requires
\[
c_{\rm lo}\le c_Er_{\min}q_{\rm cap}\Delta\tau.
\]
This is not sufficient: without an integrable summary or integration engagement, \(\mathcal A_E=0\), and this mechanism produces no change regardless of calendar time. Proved positive lower bounds on summaries and engagement can yield a corresponding lower bound on \(E_b\). This specification does not assume sustained engagement for every user.

**Joint magnitude and time limits.** If the initial value is the anchor and the pool profile is fixed at \(\bar z\), let
\(\mathfrak a_i=\rho_b|\bar z_i-b_{0,i}|\). By [Appendix B.8](#sec-b-8),
\[
|\Delta b_i|=\mathfrak a_i(1-e^{-E_b}).
\]
For a specified absolute difference \(d_i>0\), reaching at least \(d_i\) in finite time requires \(\mathfrak a_i>d_i\) and
\[
E_b\ge-\log(1-d_i/\mathfrak a_i).
\]
Together these are also sufficient under the fixed-target conditions above. If \(\mathfrak a_i=d_i>0\), finite \(E_b\) cannot reach it; if \(\mathfrak a_i<d_i\), no amount of experience can reach it. Persistently low profiles and attenuation by \(\rho_b\) can therefore impose a genuine magnitude limit that changing \(H_b\) alone cannot remove. These conditions do not declare an arbitrary \(d_i\) to be a human minimum perceptible difference.

<a id="sec-b-11-1"></a>

#### B.11.1 Triangular Coupling with Fixed Activity and Registration

Compare two admissible instances differing only in \(H_b\), with the same abstract-time coordinate and the following fixed data:

- Identical \(\mu_E,\delta_E,Q_{\max},\lambda_D\) and initial \(Q_E,\Xi_E,D_E\).
- Identical numerical functions \(u_E(\tau),j_E(\tau)\).
- Identical experience-registration times, order, fractions \(c_l\), and deduplication/discard decisions.

The emotional profiles \(z^E,z_l\) need not be identical. From [Section 10.3](#sec-10-3),
\[
\dot Q_E
=j_E(1-Q_E/Q_{\max})-\lambda_DQ_E
-\frac{\mu_Eu_EQ_E}{\delta_E+Q_E},
\]
and
\[
Q_E^+=e^{-c_l/Q_{\max}}Q_E^-
+Q_{\max}(1-e^{-c_l/Q_{\max}}),
\]
both omit \(H_b,b,P^E,z^E,z_l\). Solution uniqueness and event-by-event concatenation therefore give identical \(Q_E(\tau)\) in the two instances. Integrating the same \(r_E\) and discard rate then gives identical \(\Xi_E,D_E\) and
\[
\boxed{\mathcal A_E[H_b]=\mathcal A_E,\qquad
E_b[H_b]=\frac{\mu_E\mathcal A_E}{H_b}.}
\]
This is a triangular dependence of the vector field on experience amount and experience content. Even if changing \(b\) changes \(e\) and new profiles, profiles enter only \(P^E\) and do not alone change \(Q_E\).

The explicit \(H_b\) interval in [Appendix B.11](#sec-b-11) is therefore not merely approximate: under these conditions, it is exact for integration weight / fixed-target completion fraction. It does not follow that final \(b\) or \(y\) depends on \(H_b\) through only one exponential, because profiles and other emotion states may still change.

Using the same activity-policy function does not mean identical numerical inputs if that function produces different actual \(u_E\) from emotion or fatigue. The same applies when registration eligibility, fractions, completion times, or \(j_E\) vary with state. The implicit set is then still required. Such feedback is determined by functional dependence, not by whether the code resides inside a module or in an external adapter. State feedback permitted by [Appendix A.1](#sec-a-1) must still satisfy its regularity conditions.

<a id="sec-b-11-2"></a>

#### B.11.2 Time Conversion Does Not Imply Linear Multiplication of Effective Experience

Distinguish three operations:

**A. Change only the abstract-time unit.** Let \(\widetilde\tau=a\tau+c\) and consistently convert all time-related quantities under [Section 14](#sec-14). Then
\[
\widetilde T_{\rm ref}=T_{\rm ref}/a,\quad
\widetilde\mu_E=\mu_E/a,\quad
\widetilde{\mathcal A}_E=a\mathcal A_E,
\quad
\widetilde E_b=E_b.
\]
The state at each corresponding real-world time is unchanged. This does not accelerate personality, emotion, or fatigue.

**B. Fix the complete abstract trajectory and change only its real-world labels.** Without loss of generality, take \(\tau_0=0\) and fix
\[
a_E^{\rm occ}(\tau)
=u_E(\tau)\frac{Q_E(\tau)}{\delta_E+Q_E(\tau)}.
\]
The same real-world interval \(D_{\rm real}\) originally corresponds to
\(L=D_{\rm real}/T_{\rm ref}\). Halving only \(T_{\rm ref}\) instead reads the same abstract trajectory through \(2L\). Then
\[
E_{\rm new}-2E_{\rm old}
=\frac{\mu_E}{H_b}
\left[
\int_L^{2L}a_E^{\rm occ}(\tau)d\tau
-\int_0^L a_E^{\rm occ}(\tau)d\tau
\right].
\]
The amount doubles exactly only if the two integration amounts are equal. Equal-duration intervals need not have identical activity, pool amounts, or effective experience. Exhaustion of finite data, mode switches, and nonstationary registration can break this equality. This operation also changes the real-world frequency of abstract events; it is not a change to personality speed alone under a fixed real-world life history.

**C. Fix the real-world activity history, change only \(T_{\rm ref}\), and retain abstract rates.** This defines different real-world dynamics. Every layer's real-world rate changes, event and activity positions in abstract time must be remapped, and pool amounts and \(\overline a_E\) generally change as well. For the same \(\mu_E,H_b,D_{\rm real}\), if the old \(\overline a_E>0\),
\[
\frac{E_{\rm new}}{E_{\rm old}}
=
\frac{T_{\rm ref,old}}{T_{\rm ref,new}}
\frac{\overline a_{E,\rm new}}{\overline a_{E,\rm old}}.
\]
Thus halving \(T_{\rm ref}\) alone does not imply doubling \(E_b\); the average integration fraction must also remain unchanged. This ratio is undefined if the old integrated amount is zero.

Assigning a calendar interval a fixed length in \(\tau\) already specifies an illustrative \(T_{\rm ref}\). An illustrative conversion is neither human calibration nor a real-clock connection.

<a id="sec-b-12"></a>

### B.12 Limits of Baseline Adaptation's Effects on Emotional Responses

**Mathematical result, restricted to fixed ongoing conditions.** Under fixed \(\alpha_i,A_i,b_i\) and \(K_i=0\) in [Appendix B.4](#sec-b-4),
\[
e_i^*=
\frac{(1-\alpha_i)(1-\kappa)b_i+\alpha_i A_i}
{1-\kappa(1-\alpha_i)}.
\]
Keeping \(A_i\) as that section's continuous response target, its baseline sensitivity is
\[
\boxed{\frac{\partial e_i^*}{\partial b_i}
=\frac{(1-\alpha_i)(1-\kappa)}
{1-\kappa(1-\alpha_i)}\in[0,1].}
\]
If \(e_i^*\) lies strictly inside the moving constraint box, \(x_i^*=e_i^*\), and the same local sensitivity applies. When \(\alpha_i=1\) and this interior condition holds, sensitivity is zero: the baseline has changed, but the internal intensity read for that ongoing emotion has not. Active box boundaries, active regulation, or changes in appraisal require the complete coupling; the zero-sensitivity conclusion does not apply.

**Regulation and box boundaries change this conclusion.** Fix \(K_i\ge0\), \(0<\alpha_i\le1\), and \(A_i>b_i\). On the equilibrium branch with active positive part in [Appendix B.4](#sec-b-4), let
\[
B_i=
\frac{(1-\kappa)[\lambda_{e,i}(1-\alpha_i)+K_i]}
{\lambda_{e,i}[1-\kappa(1-\alpha_i)]+(1-\kappa)K_i}.
\]
Taking the partial derivative of that section's formula with respect to \(b_i\) gives
\[
\frac{\partial e_i^*}{\partial b_i}=B_i.
\]
In particular, when \(\alpha_i=1,K_i>0\),
\[
B_i=\frac{(1-\kappa)K_i}{\lambda_{e,i}+(1-\kappa)K_i}>0.
\]
The claim that an attention fraction of one makes baseline differences invisible therefore requires \(K_i=0\).

If the lower or upper clip boundary at equilibrium remains active in an open neighborhood, then
\[
\frac{\partial x_i^*}{\partial b_i}
=\ell_i[(1-\kappa)+\kappa B_i],
\]
because both box boundaries have derivative \(\ell_i\) with respect to \(m_i\), and
\(m_i^*=(1-\kappa)b_i+\kappa e_i^*\). Only on the interior branch is
\(\partial x_i^*/\partial b_i=B_i\) valid; a single derivative generally cannot be used directly at a branch-switching point. For \(K_i=0\), the same holds after replacing \(B_i\) by the preceding expression. Actual automatic-regulation \(K_i\) depends in turn on state, so these partial derivatives with fixed \(K_i\) are not total derivatives of the complete feedback loop.

Here \(\alpha_i\) is the effective fraction of continuous sources on that axis, including the specified masks and source intensities. It cannot be determined merely from a subjective description of being highly focused. Which situations reveal baseline differences depends on their regulation, box-boundary, and appraisal conditions. This special case does not show that all strong-attention situations lack personality-expression differences.

Learning only \(b\) guarantees adaptability of certain baselines and subsequent backgrounds, not noticeable changes in intensity, peaks, regulation efficacy, or recovery speed in every situation. A small personality baseline, small expression differences, and completely frozen learning are distinct. Mathematically nonzero adaptation also does not establish validity of human-like expression.

\(g_i\), recovery rates, and regulation coefficients remain nonlearnable. Extensions require separately defined learning targets, domains, updates, and stability conditions, and must obey the ownership boundary in [Section 1.4](#sec-1-4). Extension needs do not give this specification authority to update memory.

<a id="sec-b-13"></a>

### B.13 Endpoint Quantization Effects Under a Strict Constraint Box

Let \(q_y=0.001\) and consider the resting-equilibrium reading \(Y_i^{\rm eq}(b_*)\) in [Appendix B.8](#sec-b-8), namely \(x=m=b_*\). Under this specification's \(0<\ell_i\le1-q_y\),
\[
Y_i^{\rm eq}(0)=0,\qquad
\boxed{0<b_{*,i}\le q_y\ \Longrightarrow\ 
Y_i^{\rm eq}(b_*)=q_y.}
\]
Proof: \(0<L_i=\ell_i b_{*,i}<q_y\), so \(K_i^{\min}=1\). The interval width is at least \(q_y\), hence \(K_i^{\max}\ge1\). The nearest-grid index can only be 0 or 1, and inward clamping makes either result 1. More generally, whenever \(m_i>0\), every admissible grid output is at least \(q_y\); larger internal values may yield larger outputs.

Thus, on a zero-anchor axis, an arbitrarily small positive baseline can produce the smallest nonzero resting reading. This is not evidence of a full grid step of personality change, nor a basis for judging human perceptibility. Conversely, reading \(q_y\) does not uniquely determine \(b_{*,i}\); quantization error and the constraint box must also be considered. This does not make every experienced axis positive, since its actual summary, integration amount, and baseline may still be zero.

The upper endpoint has a symmetric grid effect:
\[
Y_i^{\rm eq}(1)=1,\qquad
1-q_y\le b_{*,i}<1\ \Longrightarrow\
Y_i^{\rm eq}(b_*)=1-q_y.
\]
Since \(1-q_y\le U_i<1\), we have \(K_i^{\max}=999\), while \(K_i^{\min}\le999\). Both endpoint effects arise from quantization and strict spatial constraints, not new psychological thresholds.

If \(L_i>0\), output 0 violates \(y_i\ge L_i\), and the three-decimal grid contains no value between 0 and \(q_y\). Under the specified rules, the output therefore cannot also be required to remain zero there. The required three-decimal precision and output-inside-box guarantee are retained, without adding decimal places, relaxing box boundaries, or zeroing small internal values.

This result concerns only inward quantization of mood axes. Fatigue uses ordinary nearest-grid-point quantization and has no corresponding effect imposed by a long-term mood lower bound. All small internal changes remain stored at full precision.

<a id="sec-c"></a>

## Appendix C Numerical Stability, Finite Precision, and Error

<a id="sec-c-1"></a>

### C.1 Local Stability and Stiffness Limits of Frozen Stepping

Interval preservation, convergence under step refinement, and local stability at fixed step size are distinct properties. This section analyzes the synchronous frozen map in [Section 16.2](#sec-16-2), without changing the continuous emotion equations. Let \(z\) include all independent states feeding back in the current reduced system; exclude truly fixed parameters and accounting quantities without feedback. Assume constant inputs, no events or mode switches, and, in an open neighborhood of equilibrium \(z_*\),
\[
\dot z_i=a_i(z)[q_i(z)-z_i],\qquad
a_i(z)>0,\quad a_i,q_i\in C^1,\quad z_{*,i}=q_i(z_*).
\]
This covers the reduced \(e,m,w,f\) system on one smooth branch, but not threshold crossings or budget exhaustion, and not directly the coupled L1 stepping of [Section 16.2](#sec-16-2). If other feedback states are omitted, the results apply only to that reduced system.

Let \(h>0\) be the computational step size in abstract time, and define
\[
\Phi_{h,i}(z)=e^{-a_i(z)h}z_i+
[1-e^{-a_i(z)h}]q_i(z),\qquad
J=D\mathcal V(z_*).
\]
Since \(q_i-z_i=0\) at equilibrium, the additional terms from differentiating \(a_i\) cancel, giving
\[
\boxed{
M_h=D\Phi_h(z_*)=I+\mathsf D_hJ,\qquad
\mathsf D_h=\operatorname{diag}
\left(\frac{1-e^{-a_i^*h}}{a_i^*}\right),\quad a_i^*=a_i(z_*).
}
\]
If \(\operatorname{spr}(M_h)<1\), the discrete map is locally exponentially stable at that equilibrium. A value greater than 1 implies instability; equality to 1 is inconclusive under the linear criterion. Here \(\operatorname{spr}\) is the spectral radius. A Hurwitz continuous \(J\) does not imply this condition for every \(h\). See [M2, continuous and discrete linearization theorems](https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-243j-dynamics-of-nonlinear-systems-fall-2003/da3ee58a1bcd2becd6920e201eeea7b1_lec8_6243_2003.pdf).

**An explicit sufficient step-size bound.** If \(J\) is Hurwitz, let
\[
P=\int_0^\infty e^{J^\top v}e^{Jv}\,dv,\qquad
J^\top P+PJ=-I,\quad P=P^\top>0,
\]
and write \(a_{\max}=\max_i a_i^*\), using the spectral norm \(\|\cdot\|_2\) throughout. Properties of this integral and the Lyapunov equation are general mathematical tools; see [M1](https://ee363.stanford.edu/archive/lectures/lq-lyap.pdf). The following bound is derived specifically for this frozen map:
\[
\boxed{
0<h<
h_{\rm suff}:=
\frac{1}{\|P\|_2\|J\|_2(a_{\max}+\|J\|_2)}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\operatorname{spr}(M_h)<1.
}
\]
Proof: from \(0\le h-(1-e^{-ah})/a\le ah^2/2\),
\[
\|\mathsf D_h\|_2\le h,\qquad
\|\mathsf D_h-hI\|_2\le a_{\max}h^2/2.
\]
Expand \(M_h^\top PM_h-P\), separate \(h(J^\top P+PJ)\) from the first-order term, and bound the remainder by norms to obtain
\[
M_h^\top PM_h-P
\preceq
\left[-h+\|P\|_2\|J\|_2
(a_{\max}+\|J\|_2)h^2\right]I.
\]
Strict negative definiteness of the right-hand side gives the result. The bound may be conservative and is not an optimal step size. It guarantees numerical stability near a fixed smooth equilibrium, not a whole-trajectory error bound, global attraction, or behavior across branches. Under time rescaling \(\tau'=c\tau\), \(J'=J/c,\ a_{\max}'=a_{\max}/c,\ P'=cP\), hence \(h_{\rm suff}'=c h_{\rm suff}\), consistent with the computational time units of the same process.

**Rapidly varying fatigue-regulation coefficients.** On the active single-axis branch of [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8), let \(d=e-m\), \(s_f=1+\rho_ff\), and \(C=s_f^\chi\). Then
\[
U_S=\frac{uC\delta_r}{(\delta_r+CS)^2}
\le\frac{uC}{\delta_r},\qquad
H_s=\frac{\eta d}{s_f}U_S
\le\frac{\eta u d\,s_f^{\chi-1}}{\delta_r}
\le\frac{\eta}{\delta_r}.
\]
where \(d\in(0,1]\), \(u\le1\), and \(\chi\le1\). As \(S\downarrow0\) with a positive threshold, \(d\) may remain close to that threshold, making \(H_s\) large. The frozen emotion-decay coefficient in [Section 16.2](#sec-16-2) is \(\lambda_e+K\), whereas the continuous Jacobian also contains \(H_s\). Admissible step size cannot be judged from the frozen coefficient alone. Coarse steps may alternate across the threshold even while each step preserves \([0,1]\).

Small \(hH_s\) is only a scale indication for one coupling term, not a sufficient stability condition for the complete system. This specification does not make “\(\lesssim1\)” with undefined constants a normative rule. Finite positive \(\delta_r\) still ensures well-definedness, but supplies no fixed step size uniformly valid for all \(\delta_r\downarrow0\). Absence of oscillation with default values, finitely many sources, and particular step sizes does not establish safety for arbitrary step sizes. An epsilon must not rewrite psychological thresholds, and numerical oscillations must not be treated as established human emotional oscillations. The segmentation and vanishing-step semantics of [Section 16.3](#sec-16-3) still apply.

**Step-size counterexample at the specified low-fatigue equilibrium.** The exact bound for \(f_-\) in [Appendix D.10](#sec-d-10) gives \(a_1>213009\). For that parameter set \(\chi=0\), \(\nu=k_f\le1,\lambda_m=1/200\), so
\[
\lambda_e+h_{\rm Jac}=a_1-\lambda_m-\nu>213000.
\]
Here \(h_{\rm Jac}\) is a Jacobian coefficient from [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6), distinct from computational step size \(h\). The frozen emotion coefficient satisfies
\(a_e=\lambda_e+K\le11\). For the three-dimensional \((e,m,f)\) map, write
\(d_j(h)=(1-e^{-a_j^*h})/a_j^*\). Then
\[
\operatorname{tr}M_h
=3-d_e(h)(\lambda_e+h_{\rm Jac})
-d_m(h)\lambda_m-d_f(h)\nu.
\]
Choose the purely computational step size \(h=1/2000\). From
\(d_e(h)\ge h-11h^2/2>0\), exactly,
\[
\operatorname{tr}M_h
<3-213000\,\frac1{2000}\left(1-\frac{11}{4000}\right)
=-\frac{825657}{8000}<-3.
\]
The sum of the absolute values of the three eigenvalues is at least the absolute trace, so
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{spr}(M_h)>
\frac{825657}{24000}>1.}
\]
Thus this step size remains locally unstable at that equilibrium for the specified synchronous frozen method. \(w\) has no feedback; including it does not remove these eigenvalues. This is an analytical finite-step counterexample, not instability of the continuous emotion equilibrium or real-time calibration.

Apparent convergence of a finite trajectory or finite-precision readings does not replace the local stability criterion. [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5) distinguishes a fixed point of the entire vector from stagnation of some components.

<a id="sec-c-2"></a>

### C.2 Exact Local Step-Size Criterion for the Single-Axis Frozen Map

\(h_{\rm suff}\) is an explicit sufficient guarantee, not the maximum stable step size or a mandatory operational step size. For a fixed smooth equilibrium, \(\operatorname{spr}(M_h)\) in [Appendix C.1](#sec-c-1) gives a more direct linear criterion. This section reduces the three-dimensional subsystem of [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8) to finitely many real inequalities, rather than leaving only an instruction to compute eigenvalues. It adds no psychological parameters and changes no integration method.

**Scope and frozen rates.** Retain all assumptions of [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8) and its actual equilibrium \(z_*=(e_*,m_*,f_*)\). Here \(H_J\) denotes that section's local coefficient \(h\), reserving \(h>0\) for computational step size. Let
\[
H_J=\frac{\eta}{s_f}(U+dU_S),\qquad
K=\frac{\eta U}{s_f},\qquad
L=\lambda D_\alpha+H_J(1-\kappa).
\]
Evaluate every right-hand side at equilibrium. The three rates actually frozen in [Section 16.2](#sec-16-2) are
\[
\bar a_e=\lambda+K,\qquad
\bar a_m=\lambda_m,\qquad
\bar a_f=k_f=a_f\omega U+b_fq(1-U)>0.
\]
They differ from the absolute values of the Jacobian's diagonal entries. For emotion, do not replace
\(\bar a_e\) by \(\lambda+H_J\); for fatigue, do not replace
\(\bar a_f\) by \(\nu\), which contains \(U_f\) feedback. Define
\[
d_j(h)=\frac{1-e^{-\bar a_jh}}{\bar a_j}>0,\quad
j\in\{e,m,f\},\qquad
M_{3,h}=I+\operatorname{diag}(d_e,d_m,d_f)J_3.
\]

Let
\[
\mathsf B_1=d_e(\lambda+H_J)+d_m\lambda_m+d_f\nu,
\]
\[
\mathsf B_2=d_ed_m\lambda_m L
+d_ed_f[(\lambda+H_J)\nu-gr]
+d_md_f\lambda_m\nu,
\]
\[
\mathsf B_3=d_ed_md_f\,a_3
=d_ed_md_f\lambda_m[\nu L-gr(1-\kappa)].
\]
From the trace, second-order principal minors, and determinant,
\[
p_h(\zeta):=\det(\zeta I-M_{3,h})
=(\zeta-1)^3+\mathsf B_1(\zeta-1)^2+
\mathsf B_2(\zeta-1)+\mathsf B_3.
\]
Define four dimensionless coefficients:
\[
\mathsf C_0=\mathsf B_3,\quad
\mathsf C_1=2\mathsf B_2-3\mathsf B_3,\quad
\mathsf C_2=4\mathsf B_1-4\mathsf B_2+3\mathsf B_3,
\]
\[
\mathsf C_3=8-4\mathsf B_1+2\mathsf B_2-\mathsf B_3.
\]

**Proposition.** In exact arithmetic,
\[
\boxed{
\operatorname{spr}(M_{3,h})<1
\iff
\mathsf C_0,\mathsf C_1,\mathsf C_2,\mathsf C_3>0
\ \text{and}\ 
\mathsf C_2\mathsf C_1>\mathsf C_3\mathsf C_0.
}
\]
The right-hand side is necessary and sufficient for strict Schur stability of the linearized matrix. Under the stated smooth-equilibrium assumptions, it gives local exponential stability of the nonlinear numerical map. If not all conditions hold, the immediate conclusion is only that strict Schur stability fails. Spectral radius greater than 1 and exactly equal to 1 must still be treated separately under [Appendix C.1](#sec-c-1).

**Proof.** Use the purely algebraic bilinear transformation
\[
v=\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta+1},\qquad
\zeta=\frac{1+v}{1-v},\qquad
\Re v=\frac{|\zeta|^2-1}{|\zeta+1|^2}.
\]
Thus \(|\zeta|<1\) is equivalent to \(\Re v<0\); \(\zeta=-1\) must be excluded separately. This is a general variable transformation; see [M3, Algorithms](https://www.mathworks.com/help/signal/ref/bilinear.html). Its use is proved directly here, without introducing a new time-discretization method or real-clock mapping.

Expand
\[
(1-v)^3p_h\!\left(\frac{1+v}{1-v}\right)
=\mathsf C_3v^3+\mathsf C_2v^2+\mathsf C_1v+\mathsf C_0.
\],
where \(\mathsf C_3=-p_h(-1)\). If all roots of \(p_h\) lie inside the unit circle, its real monic cubic form and pairing of real and conjugate roots imply \(p_h(-1)<0\), hence \(\mathsf C_3>0\). Conversely, the positive-coefficient conditions also exclude \(\zeta=-1\), and the transformed expression equals 8 at \(v=1\), introducing no additional root. Applying positivity of the first column of the cubic Routh table,
\[
\mathsf C_3,\quad \mathsf C_2,\quad
\frac{\mathsf C_2\mathsf C_1-\mathsf C_3\mathsf C_0}{\mathsf C_2},
\quad\mathsf C_0
\],
gives the equivalence. This is an algebraic derivation for the specified map, not a new assumption about human behavior.

**Sign of the constant term for all step sizes and simplification of the conditions.** At the same fixed smooth equilibrium, for every finite \(h>0\),
\[
\operatorname{sgn}\mathsf C_0(h)
=\operatorname{sgn}p_h(1)=\operatorname{sgn}a_3,
\]
because \(\mathsf C_0=p_h(1)=d_ed_md_fa_3\) and all three \(d_j\) are strictly positive. Therefore:

- If \(a_3>0\), then \(\mathsf C_0>0\) holds for every finite positive step size. Strict Schur stability is then equivalent to the other four conditions:
  \[
  \mathsf C_1>0,\quad\mathsf C_2>0,\quad\mathsf C_3>0,\quad
  \mathsf C_2\mathsf C_1>\mathsf C_3\mathsf C_0.
  \]
  \(\mathsf C_0\) must remain in the final condition; it cannot be treated as constant or deleted. Hurwitz \(J_3\) is sufficient for \(a_3>0\), but \(a_3>0\) alone guarantees neither Hurwitz \(J_3\) nor the other four conditions.
- If \(a_3<0\), then \(p_h(1)<0\), while the monic cubic tends to positive infinity as real \(\zeta\to+\infty\). The intermediate value theorem gives a real root \(\mu>1\), so every finite positive step size satisfies \(\operatorname{spr}(M_{3,h})>1\). This is local numerical instability at that same equilibrium, not unboundedness of every trajectory.
- If \(a_3=0\), then \(p_h(1)=0\), so every finite positive step size has eigenvalue \(+1\) and cannot be strictly Schur stable. Other roots may be inside or outside the unit circle; this equality alone does not determine nonlinear local stability.

These are algebraic consequences of [Appendix C.2](#sec-c-2), requiring no grid observations. When \(h=0\), \(M_{3,0}=I\), outside the positive-step conclusions. Eliminating one automatically satisfied condition at a Hurwitz equilibrium does not prove the others over an entire step-size interval, nor that an observed \(-1\) crossing is \(h_{\rm exit}\).

**Relation between three and four dimensions.** \(w\) does not feed back into \((e,m,f)\). If \(F\) is differentiable at equilibrium, ordering states as \((e,m,f,w)\) gives
\[
M_{4,h}=
\begin{pmatrix}
M_{3,h}&0\\
v_h^\top&e^{-\lambda_xh}
\end{pmatrix},\qquad
v_h^\top=(1-e^{-\lambda_xh})D_{(e,m,f)}F.
\]
and hence
\[
\operatorname{spr}(M_{4,h})
=\max\{\operatorname{spr}(M_{3,h}),e^{-\lambda_xh}\},
\]
For every finite \(h>0\), the latter term is strictly below 1, so the three-dimensional criterion suffices for this smooth four-dimensional reduced system. If \(F\) lies exactly at a clip corner, that four-dimensional Jacobian must not be assumed to exist. When the three-dimensional map is locally exponentially stable, \(w\) is still locally exponentially stable by its Lipschitz cascade relation:
\[
|\Delta w_n|
\le r_x^n|\Delta w_0|+
(1-r_x)L_F\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}r_x^{n-1-j}\|\Delta z_j\|,
\qquad r_x=e^{-\lambda_xh}<1.
\]
This convolution of exponentially decaying sequences is bounded by a slower positive exponential decay rate. Here \(n\) is only the numerical iteration index; the corresponding elapsed abstract time is \(nh\).

“Four-dimensional” is not the fixed dimension of the complete module. Shared regulation across axes, ongoing personality or sentiments updates, state-dependent sources, event jumps, and other feedback states require the corresponding complete map and its dimension; this four-dimensional formula does not apply directly.

**Stable-step set and its first interval.** For fixed equilibrium and parameters, define
\[
\mathcal H_*=\{h>0:\operatorname{spr}(M_{3,h})<1\}.
\]
Continuity makes this set open. If \(J_3\) is Hurwitz, it contains
\((0,h_{\rm suff})\), using the sufficient bound from [Appendix C.1](#sec-c-1) for this three-dimensional system. Define the endpoint of the stable interval extending from zero as
\[
h_{\rm exit}=
\sup\{H>0:(0,H)\subseteq\mathcal H_*\}\in(0,\infty].
\]
This is the right endpoint of the first connected interval. Until \(\mathcal H_*\) is proved to be one interval, it cannot be identified with the supremum of all stable step sizes. Nor can the exact endpoint be inferred from the first violation on a finite grid. Monotonicity of spectral radius in \(h\) has not been proved either.

Neither the linear criterion nor \(h_{\rm suff}\) guarantees trajectory accuracy. A very large locally stable step may substantially distort transients. Stability for each fixed step size also does not alone establish stability of matrix products under arbitrary step switching or time-varying inputs. The numerical-consistency and segmentation semantics of [Section 16.3](#sec-16-3) still apply.

Under pure rescaling \(\tau'=c\tau,\ h'=ch\),
\(\bar a_j'=\bar a_j/c,\ d_j'=c\,d_j,\ J_3'=J_3/c\),
so \(M_{3,h'}'=M_{3,h}\). Every \(\mathsf B_j,\mathsf C_j\) and stability decision is unchanged, with \(\mathcal H_*'=c\mathcal H_*\). These are properties of the computational representation, not natural emotional time constants.

<a id="sec-c-3"></a>

### C.3 Interpreting Decisions at Approximate Equilibria and Finite Precision

The \(I+\mathsf D_hJ\) formulas in [Appendices C.1–C.2](#sec-c-1) are evaluated at actual equilibria. Given only an approximate state \(\widehat z\), its residual cannot be discarded before calling the resulting matrix the exact equilibrium linearization.

On the same smooth branch as [Appendix C.1](#sec-c-1), let
\[
d_i(z;h)=\frac{1-e^{-a_i(z)h}}{a_i(z)}
=\int_0^h e^{-a_i(z)v}\,dv,\qquad
\Phi_h(z)=z+\mathsf D_h(z)\mathcal V(z).
\]
Termwise differentiation gives the complete formula at any such state:
\[
\boxed{
D\Phi_h(z)=I+\mathsf D_h(z)D\mathcal V(z)
+\operatorname{diag}(\mathcal V(z))G_h(z),\qquad
(G_h)_{ij}=\frac{\partial d_i(z;h)}{\partial z_j}.
}
\]
From
\[
\left|\frac{\partial d_i}{\partial a_i}\right|
=\int_0^h v e^{-a_iv}\,dv\le h^2/2
\],
the residual term is bounded by
\[
\left\|\operatorname{diag}(\mathcal V(z))G_h(z)\right\|_2
\le \frac{h^2}{2}\|\mathcal V(z)\|_\infty\|Da(z)\|_2.
\]
It vanishes exactly at equilibrium, but need not vanish at an approximate equilibrium. Equilibrium is sufficient, not necessary, for its vanishing. For example, when \(Da=0\), \(G_h=0\), so the term vanishes even if \(\mathcal V\ne0\). Even a small term still requires information about equilibrium existence, location error, and the relevant smooth branch before conclusions about an approximate matrix can be transferred to the actual equilibrium. At a general nonequilibrium state, eigenvalues of a pointwise Jacobian do not determine stability of an entire nonautonomous trajectory.

**Sufficient certificate with a matrix-error bound.** For a specified actual equilibrium matrix \(M=M_h(z_*)\), suppose an approximate matrix \(\widehat M\) and proved error bound satisfy
\[
\|M-\widehat M\|_2\le\varepsilon_M.
\]
If one symmetric matrix \(P>0\) and \(\gamma>0\) satisfy
\[
\widehat M^\top P\widehat M-P\preceq-\gamma I,
\]
and
\[
\boxed{
\gamma>\|P\|_2
\left(2\|\widehat M\|_2\varepsilon_M+\varepsilon_M^2\right),
}
\],
then the actual \(M\) is strictly Schur stable. Proof: write
\(M=\widehat M+E\) and expand \(M^\top PM-P\). The sum of spectral norms of the two cross terms is at most
\(2\|P\|_2\|\widehat M\|_2\varepsilon_M\), and the quadratic term is at most
\(\|P\|_2\varepsilon_M^2\). Strict negative definiteness therefore remains, and the discrete Lyapunov criterion yields the result. [M1, discrete-time results](https://ee363.stanford.edu/archive/lectures/lq-lyap.pdf)
