This is a conditional sufficient certificate, not a supplied \(\varepsilon_M\) for every operating point. The error bound must cover equilibrium-location, parameter, coefficient, and exponential-evaluation errors actually present. A small residual alone generally does not bound location error. If \(P>0\) and the matrix inequality are themselves given in finite precision, valid upper and lower bounds are also needed; displayed numbers appearing positive or negative are not proof.

The scalar inequalities in [Appendix C.2](#sec-c-2) work similarly: intervals containing the actual coefficients that all lie strictly on the required positive side prove strict Schur stability. An interval crossing zero means only that these bounds are inconclusive. Rational substitution at a few endpoints or midpoints does not prove the sign over the whole interval without a separate monotonicity or interval-enclosure proof. This distinguishes exact rational point evaluations from the rational upper and lower bounds covering an entire root interval in [Appendix D.10](#sec-d-10).

An eigenvalue computation showing \(\operatorname{spr}(\widehat M)\) approximately equal to 1 within computational error does not determine which side contains the actual spectral radius. Decay of one particular error vector also does not exclude instability in other directions. Here \(\varepsilon_M\) is only a numerical-error bound; it changes no emotion threshold, output grid spacing, or psychological state, and is not a test scenario or procedure.

<a id="sec-c-4"></a>

### C.4 The \(-1\) Spectral Boundary and Alternating Numerical Instability

Fix the same actual equilibrium, psychological parameters, and external sources as in [Appendix C.2](#sec-c-2), varying only computational step size \(h\). The continuous equilibrium does not change with this computational parameter. All \(\mathsf B_j,\mathsf C_j,p_h\) below are defined along that fixed equilibrium, without changes from reselecting sources or equilibria.

From \(\mathsf C_3(h)=-p_h(-1)\), immediately,
\[
\mathsf C_3(h)=0\iff -1\text{ is an eigenvalue of }M_{3,h}\text{}.
\]
If \(\mathsf C_3(h)<0\), then \(p_h(-1)>0\). The monic cubic tends to negative infinity along the negative real axis, so it has a real root \(\mu<-1\). Hence
\[
\boxed{\mathsf C_3(h)<0\Longrightarrow
\operatorname{spr}(M_{3,h})>1.}
\]
The converse fails: other Schur conditions may fail first. \(\mathsf C_3=0\) alone determines neither crossing direction, root multiplicity, nor nonlinear periodic orbits.

**Sufficient conditions for a simple root and transverse crossing.** Suppose a finite \(h_c>0\) satisfies
\[
\mathsf C_3(h_c)=0,\qquad
\mathsf C_0(h_c),\mathsf C_1(h_c),\mathsf C_2(h_c)>0.
\]
Then \(-1\) is a simple root and the other two eigenvalues lie strictly inside the unit circle. Proof: the bilinearly transformed expression reduces to
\[
\mathsf C_2v^2+\mathsf C_1v+\mathsf C_0.
\]
The two roots of this positive-coefficient quadratic have negative real parts; transforming back gives strict Schur stability of the other roots. Differentiating the original polynomial also gives the identity
\[
2\partial_\zeta p_h(-1)=\mathsf C_2(h)+3\mathsf C_3(h).
\]
Thus, at the critical point,
\(\partial_\zeta p_{h_c}(-1)=\mathsf C_2(h_c)/2>0\), proving simplicity.

The implicit function theorem gives a nearby real differentiable eigenvalue branch \(\mu(h)\), with
\[
p_h(\mu(h))=0,\qquad
\boxed{\mu'(h_c)=
\frac{2\mathsf C_3'(h_c)}{\mathsf C_2(h_c)}.}
\]
If additionally \(\mathsf C_3'(h_c)<0\), then as \(h\) moves from the left of \(h_c\) to the right, this root crosses from inside the unit circle through \(-1\) into \(\mu<-1\). Strictness and continuity of the remaining conditions make the map locally exponentially stable in a sufficiently small left neighborhood and unstable in a right neighborhood. A positive derivative reverses the direction; a zero derivative makes this first-order crossing criterion inconclusive.

This is a local boundary result. Without proving that the whole \((0,h_c)\) belongs to \(\mathcal H_*\), such a \(h_c\) cannot be called the first \(h_{\rm exit}\). One observed criterion switch on a finite grid is not a proof over that interval.

**Computable definition of crossing speed.** Let
\[
E_j(h)=e^{-\bar a_jh}=d_j'(h),\qquad j\in\{e,m,f\},
\]
\[
Z_{em}=\lambda_mL,\quad
Z_{ef}=(\lambda+H_J)\nu-gr,\quad
Z_{mf}=\lambda_m\nu.
\]
All \(Z\), \(a_3\), and \(\bar a_j\) are fixed in this analysis, so
\[
\mathsf B_1'=E_e(\lambda+H_J)+E_m\lambda_m+E_f\nu,
\]
\[
\mathsf B_2'=
(E_ed_m+d_eE_m)Z_{em}+
(E_ed_f+d_eE_f)Z_{ef}+
(E_md_f+d_mE_f)Z_{mf},
\]
\[
\mathsf B_3'=a_3(E_ed_md_f+d_eE_md_f+d_ed_mE_f),
\qquad
\mathsf C_3'=-4\mathsf B_1'+2\mathsf B_2'-\mathsf B_3'.
\]
These derivatives are not replaced by finite-difference slopes. Under consistent time-unit rescaling, the critical step is multiplied by the scale factor, while \(\mathsf C_3'\) and \(\mu'\) are divided by it, leaving crossing direction unchanged.

**Interpretive limits.** The linear error component along a real eigenvector evolves as \(\mu^n\). When \(\mu<-1\), it changes sign between successive numerical steps while growing in magnitude. This is one explicit mechanism for alternating numerical oscillation; it proves neither that this mechanism is common in all large-step instabilities nor that the complete nonlinear trajectory must have period two.

A nonlinear period-doubling bifurcation additionally involves higher-order terms and nondegeneracy conditions. The critical cubic coefficient on the center manifold is extra information not obtainable from \(\mathsf C_3=0\) alone; see [M5, Theorem 6.18](https://webspace.science.uu.nl/~kouzn101/NLDV/Lect12.pdf) for general mathematical background. This specification does not complete that higher-order bifurcation classification at any operating point. Such computational-step-induced instability must not be interpreted directly as a human emotional cycle or oscillation of the continuous emotion equations.

<a id="sec-c-5"></a>

### C.5 Distinguishing Finite-Precision Stagnation from Continuous Equilibrium

**Engineering numerical representation; model-specific derivation.** [Appendices C.1–C.4](#sec-c-1) analyze the frozen map in real arithmetic. Here specification parameters and start-of-step inputs are collected as \(\pi\), with target map \(\Phi_h(z;\pi)\); stored state \(z\) is treated as an exact real number. This convention isolates one-step computation error only; it does not claim that \(z\) equals the true continuous trajectory.

Let \(\widehat\pi,\widehat h\) be the actually stored parameters/inputs and step size, all within their admissible domains. This section concerns finite arithmetic results; error notation does not conceal overflow, NaN, or inadmissible values. Define
\[
\epsilon_h^{\rm rep}(z)
:=\Phi_{\widehat h}(z;\widehat\pi)-\Phi_h(z;\pi),
\]
\[
\boxed{
\widehat\Phi_h(z)
=R_{\rm fp}\!\left(
\Phi_{\widehat h}(z;\widehat\pi)+\epsilon_h^{\rm ar}(z)
\right)
=R_{\rm fp}\!\left(
\Phi_h(z;\pi)+\epsilon_h^{\rm num}(z)
\right),\quad
\epsilon_h^{\rm num}=\epsilon_h^{\rm rep}+\epsilon_h^{\rm ar}.
}
\]
\(\epsilon^{\rm rep}\) is the map difference due to representations of parameters, inputs, and step size. \(\epsilon^{\rm ar}\) is arithmetic error relative to the exact map of those stored values; \(R_{\rm fp}\) is final state-storage rounding. When comparing calculations at different precisions from the same stored state, if one uses an exact rational threshold and the other its binary approximation, not all differences are arithmetic error. If the target of analysis is the model defined by stored parameters and step size, set \(\pi=\widehat\pi,h=\widehat h\), giving \(\epsilon^{\rm rep}=0\).

None of these errors is an additional psychological input or state. The decomposition requires explicit arithmetic and rounding semantics; it must not assume that all operations round only once at the end. The exact real map is only the comparison reference. Total numerical error relative to the target map is \(\epsilon^{\rm num}\), distinct from its component sources. [M4](https://docs.oracle.com/cd/E19957-01/806-3568/ncg_goldberg.html) provides general background on representation, rounding, and cancellation; [Appendix C.6](#sec-c-6) derives this model's error-propagation relations.

**Semantics of identical stored values and textual round trips.** Treating \(z\) as an exact real number here means the actual finite numerical value represented by a known storage format, not declaring its display string to be the same exact real number. The provenance and representation contract in [Section 16.4](#sec-16-4) also applies to high-precision analysis. More arithmetic digits do not restore a replaced input value.

To make the distinction explicit, let \(\mathfrak F\) be the finite numerical values of a specified format, and \(\operatorname{val}_{\mathfrak F}(x)\) the real number represented by stored object \(x\). Every finite binary value is an integer times an integer power of two, hence rational. If text encoder \(\operatorname{ser}\) and same-format decoder \(\operatorname{parse}_{\mathfrak F}\) have a round-trip contract, then
\[
\operatorname{val}_{\mathfrak F}
\bigl(\operatorname{parse}_{\mathfrak F}(\operatorname{ser}(x))\bigr)
=\operatorname{val}_{\mathfrak F}(x).
\]
Separately, let \(\operatorname{dec}(s)\) denote the exact rational number written by decimal string \(s\). The preceding equation generally does not imply
\[
\operatorname{dec}(\operatorname{ser}(x))
=\operatorname{val}_{\mathfrak F}(x).
\]
The former allows the string to round back to the original floating-point value under the specified decoder; the latter requires the string's mathematical value itself to equal that value. These are different contracts. With a known codec and format, a round-trip string can recover the same finite numerical value. Text of unknown provenance, truncated text, or display-only formatting cannot masquerade as a known lossless representation. This condition does not require identical bit representations in every case: the model treats signed zero as numerical zero, while NaN and infinities remain invalid.

Using the official Python documentation as a semantic reference, constructing Decimal.from_float(x) or Decimal(x) from a finite float preserves that float's exact numerical value. Decimal(repr(x)) instead takes the decimal value of the string. They may differ and must not be interchanged without distinction in an analysis of the same stored state. If the intended target is the value specified by decimal text, constructing from that text is appropriate; decimal inputs are not categorically prohibited. [M6a: Decimal construction and from_float](https://docs.python.org/3/library/decimal.html#decimal.Decimal.from_float), [M6b: repr and floating-point round trips](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/floatingpoint.html)

Exact integer ratios and hexadecimal representations with specified formats can also express stored floating-point values. The official float.as_integer_ratio() and float.hex() documentation provides semantic references for these interfaces. This does not require Python in this module and is not code, a reproduction procedure, or a new precision threshold. [M6c: floating-point representations](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#float.as_integer_ratio)

If display text yields another state \(\widetilde z\ne z\), even exact evaluation with the same \(\pi,h\) introduces
\[
\epsilon_h^{\rm read}
:=\Phi_h(\widetilde z;\pi)-\Phi_h(z;\pi).
\]
This is not the one-step arithmetic error in [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5), where \(z\) is fixed. Only with an explicit Lipschitz constant \(L_{\Phi,h}\) over the admissible region needed for both states may it be bounded by
\(\|\epsilon_h^{\rm read}\|\le L_{\Phi,h}\|\widetilde z-z\|\). This constant is not presumed small. The formula clarifies comparison differences due to input reconstruction; it introduces no psychological dynamics.

Exact input conversion does not make subsequent multiplication, division, exponentials, or the whole-step map exact. For example, subsequent Decimal operations remain constrained by their arithmetic context. High-precision recomputation still requires its own error bounds; digit count alone does not establish exact recovery of the true value. [M6a](https://docs.python.org/3/library/decimal.html)

**Representation error of one parameter.** Retaining an exact source and actually using it are different conditions. Write \(\pi=(\theta,\xi)\), where \(\xi\) contains the remaining parameters and start-of-step inputs. If all intermediate parameter combinations below are admissible, specify the decomposition order: replace the threshold first, then the remaining quantities:
\[
\epsilon_h^{{\rm rep},\theta}
=\Phi_h(z;\widehat\theta,\xi)-\Phi_h(z;\theta,\xi),
\]
\[
\epsilon_h^{{\rm rep},{\rm rest}}
=\Phi_{\widehat h}(z;\widehat\theta,\widehat\xi)
-\Phi_h(z;\widehat\theta,\xi).
\]
Cancellation of the intermediate value gives exactly
\[
\epsilon_h^{\rm rep}
=\epsilon_h^{{\rm rep},\theta}
+\epsilon_h^{{\rm rep},{\rm rest}}.
\]
This decomposition accounts for error sources; it generates no psychological event. Reversing the replacement order generally changes individual terms but not their sum. Individual source shares in a nonlinear map are not unique causal proportions.

If the effective numerical input semantics actually retain the original rational \(\theta\), then \(\widehat\theta=\theta\) and the threshold-representation term above is zero. Merely saving a numerator/denominator while calculations still use the approximate threshold does not satisfy this condition. A zero threshold term does not make other parameter/step representation terms or arithmetic errors zero. Later finite-precision conversion of a rational for computation still incurs error relative to the declared reference. Renaming an error category does not remove final numerical error.

No universal proportion of total error is assigned to any source here. Terms of opposite sign may cancel. If their sum is small or zero, a term divided by that sum may be large or undefined. The error share of threshold difference \(\sigma\) is also not directly the state-error share after a nonlinear map. This is a model-specific decomposition; [M4, Cancellation](https://docs.oracle.com/cd/E19957-01/806-3568/ncg_goldberg.html) provides general background on separating input approximation from arithmetic error.

For a stored component \(z_j\), define its rounding cell
\[
\mathcal R_j(z_j)=\{u:R_{{\rm fp},j}(u)=z_j\}.
\]
If that component uses rounding to nearest and \(z_j^-,z_j^+\) are adjacent representable numbers, the cell interior is
\[
\left(\frac{z_j^-+z_j}{2},\frac{z_j+z_j^+}{2}\right);
\]
Assign the endpoints according to the declared tie-breaking rule. At a representational endpoint without neighbors on both sides, use the original definition of \(\mathcal R_j\) directly.

Let \(\Delta_j^- =z_j-z_j^->0,\ \Delta_j^+=z_j^+-z_j>0\). For the target component formula \(\Phi_{h,j}=z_j+d_j(z;h)\mathcal V_j(z;\pi)\) from [Appendix C.3](#sec-c-3), omitting parameter notation \(d_j\), a sufficient condition is
\[
-\frac{\Delta_j^-}{2}
<d_j(z;h)\mathcal V_j(z;\pi)+\epsilon_{h,j}^{\rm num}(z)
<\frac{\Delta_j^+}{2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\widehat\Phi_{h,j}(z)=z_j.
\]
If only \(|\epsilon_{h,j}^{\rm num}|\le\bar\epsilon_j\) is known, a more conservative sufficient condition is
\[
-\frac{\Delta_j^-}{2}+\bar\epsilon_j
<d_j(z;h)\mathcal V_j(z;\pi)
<\frac{\Delta_j^+}{2}-\bar\epsilon_j.
\]
This follows because the entire total-error interval lies inside the rounding cell, allowing
\(\mathcal V_j(z)\ne0\). Actual neighbor distances on both sides avoid replacing the complete condition with an undefined “half ulp” where floating-point spacing changes.

Unchanged bits in one component therefore do not prove a zero continuous derivative or zero unrounded update. Only when every component satisfies the same stored fixed-point condition may one write
\(\widehat\Phi_h(z)=z\). If \(e\) is unchanged while \(f\) still drifts, this is partial-component stagnation, not an exact fixed point of the entire vector. Unchanged bits over finitely many updates do not establish permanent invariance.

The rounding map and its cells generally differ from the original \(C^1\) map. A rounding plateau cannot directly be identified with local stability of \(\Phi_h\). Even when the real map has an unstable direction near an actual equilibrium, a finite-precision trajectory may remain in certain rounding cells without visible growth. An observed plateau also does not prove exactly zero error projection onto the unstable direction.

\(R_{\rm fp}\) differs from the externally reported three-decimal quantization of [Section 15](#sec-15). Internals still must not retain only the final output; however, precision greater than three decimal places alone does not ensure accumulation of arbitrarily small updates over arbitrarily long times. Numerical representation must preserve the required precision and error bounds. Artificial psychological dead zones, stopping abstract time, or invented events must not conceal rounding loss. This section changes neither the continuous model, recovery rates, event doses, nor the final eleven-axis interface.

<a id="sec-c-6"></a>

### C.6 Conditioning Near Regulation Thresholds and One-Step Error Propagation

This section describes numerical-error propagation through the regulation formula, without adding a psychological threshold or changing the fatigue equation. It first gives conditional relations for a single negative axis with no other regulation demand, then states their limits. Equilibrium is not required here.

**Threshold difference and error sources.** Fix start-of-step \(e,m,f\in[0,1]\), and let
\[
\sigma=e-m-\theta,\qquad r=[\sigma]_+,\qquad
s=1+\rho_ff,\quad C=s^\chi,\quad
\delta=\delta_r>0.
\]
Take \(p^{\rm reg}=1,g^{\rm keep}=0\), with automatic regulation demand only on this axis and zero demand on the others. Let \(u=R\,u_{\max}\in[0,1]\) be the available regulation fraction fixed for this step. The equations reduce to
\[
U(r)=\frac{uCr}{\delta+Cr},\qquad K(r)=\frac{\eta U(r)}s,\qquad \eta\ge0.
\]
This channel is zero when \(u=0\); the positive part gives \(U=K=0\) when \(r=0\). Here \(r\) is demand, not the identically named feedback coefficient in the Jacobian of [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8).

**Compatibility domain of derived quantities.** In this model,
\[
s=1+\rho_ff\ge1,\qquad C=s^\chi,\qquad
0\le\chi\le1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 1\le C\le s.
\]
\(s,C\) are derived from the same \(f,\rho_f,\chi\), not independently selectable psychological parameters; \(u=R u_{\max}\) also retains the effort-allocation relation. Algebraic identities valid on positive real domains may extend to larger domains, but do not enlarge the admissible domain of the complete emotion model. Observing an identity on an extended domain does not replace the model's compatibility conditions, state constraints, or proofs.

Given approximate data \(\widetilde e,\widetilde m,\widetilde\theta\), let \(\widetilde\sigma\) be the actual subtraction result, with \(\widetilde r=[\widetilde\sigma]_+\). If
\[
|\widetilde e-e|\le E_e,\quad
|\widetilde m-m|\le E_m,\quad
|\widetilde\theta-\theta|\le E_\theta,\quad
|\widetilde\sigma-(\widetilde e-\widetilde m-\widetilde\theta)|
\le E_{\rm sub},
\],
the triangle inequality and the positive-part function's 1-Lipschitz property give
\[
\boxed{
|\widetilde r-r|\le|\widetilde\sigma-\sigma|
\le E_\sigma:=E_e+E_m+E_\theta+E_{\rm sub}.
}
\]
This allows opposite signs of \(\sigma,\widetilde\sigma\) and includes \(\sigma=0\). Under the same-stored-state comparison of [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5), \(E_e=E_m=0\) unless differences from the continuous true value are separately analyzed; the same state error must not be counted twice. The relative bound \(E_\sigma/|\sigma|\) is meaningful only for \(\sigma\ne0\) and may be large near the threshold even when absolute error is small.

Subtracting nearby numbers does not necessarily introduce a large error in that subtraction; it may expose earlier input-representation or intermediate-rounding errors. This distinction agrees with [M4, Cancellation](https://docs.oracle.com/cd/E19957-01/806-3568/ncg_goldberg.html). Small \(\sigma\) alone does not prove that every subtraction in a program is inexact.

**From demand to the regulation coefficient.** Vary only \(r\), keeping \(u,C,s,\eta,\delta\) fixed. Combining denominators gives the exact finite difference
\[
U(\widetilde r)-U(r)
=\frac{uC\delta(\widetilde r-r)}
{(\delta+C\widetilde r)(\delta+Cr)}.
\]
Thus for any nonnegative \(r,\widetilde r\),
\[
|U(\widetilde r)-U(r)|
\le\frac{uC}{\delta}|\widetilde r-r|,\qquad
|K(\widetilde r)-K(r)|
\le\frac{\eta uC}{s\delta}E_\sigma.
\]
The complete denominator gives a finer bound; \(uC/\delta\) is a global slope bound,
\[
U_r=\frac{uC\delta}{(\delta+Cr)^2}\le\frac{uC}{\delta}.
\]
The slope approaches that bound only when \(Cr/\delta\) is small. In the saturated region, \(U_r\) must not still be treated as \(uC/\delta\). These are error-channel analyses, not claims that every operation incurs maximal error or that its sign is guaranteed.

If the computed \(\widehat K\ge0\) also differs from \(K(\widetilde r)\) by
\[
|\widehat K-K(\widetilde r)|\le E_K^{\rm other},
\],
then
\[
E_K^{\rm tot}:=
\frac{\eta uC}{s\delta}E_\sigma+E_K^{\rm other}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
|\widehat K-K(r)|\le E_K^{\rm tot}.
\]
\(E_K^{\rm other}\) covers representation and arithmetic errors not otherwise listed here; it is not a freely negligible constant. If \(u,C,\delta\) also change, the corresponding partial derivatives are
\[
U_u=\frac{Cr}{\delta+Cr},\quad
U_C=\frac{u\delta r}{(\delta+Cr)^2},\quad
U_\delta=-\frac{uCr}{(\delta+Cr)^2}.
\]
Provided the segment connecting the two value sets remains in the admissible domain with \(\delta\ge\delta_{\min}>0\), sum each corresponding error times the supremum of the absolute partial derivative to obtain a mean-value bound on that segment. Other variables in \(K=\eta U/s\) must be included by the same chain rule. This defines a bound under boundedness conditions; it does not supply universal numerical constants for all implementations. With a shared multi-axis denominator, effort overrides, or state-dependent inputs, use the full original function rather than applying the single-axis \(U(r)\) directly.

**From coefficient to target, derivative, and one-step map.** Fix identical \(e,m,T\), satisfying \(0\le m<e\le T\le1\) and \(\lambda=\lambda_e>0\). The residual-disengagement term of [Section 16.2](#sec-16-2) is then inactive. Freezing regulation coefficient \(K\ge0\) gives
\[
\mathcal V_e(K)=\lambda(T-e)-K(e-m),\quad
a(K)=\lambda+K,\quad
q(K)=\frac{\lambda T+Km}{\lambda+K}.
\]
Write \(\Delta K=\widehat K-K\). Then
\[
\mathcal V_e(\widehat K)-\mathcal V_e(K)=-(e-m)\Delta K,
\]
\[
q(\widehat K)-q(K)
=\frac{\lambda(m-T)\Delta K}
{(\lambda+\widehat K)(\lambda+K)}.
\]
One is a derivative difference per unit of abstract time; the other is a frozen-target difference. Neither is a one-step state-update difference.

For the same exact step size \(h>0\), let
\[
\phi_h(K)=e^{-a(K)h}e+(1-e^{-a(K)h})q(K).
\]
\(\phi_h\) is only the exact solution of this scalar equation frozen at the start of the step, not the exact flow of the complete coupled emotion system. To bound every finite step size, let \(x_K(v)\) solve the frozen equation
\[
\frac{dx_K}{dv}=\lambda(T-x_K)-K(x_K-m),\qquad x_K(0)=e
\]
at \(v\in[0,h]\). \(v\) is an integration variable within this solver step, not a conversation or event index. Differentiation with respect to \(K\) and variation of constants give
\[
\frac{\partial\phi_h}{\partial K}
=-\int_0^h e^{-(\lambda+K)(h-v)}(x_K(v)-m)\,dv.
\]
Since \(q(K)\in[m,T]\) and \(e\in[m,T]\), the convex-combination solution satisfies \(x_K(v)\in[m,T]\). Let \(D=T-m\in[0,1]\). Then
\[
\left|\frac{\partial\phi_h}{\partial K}\right|
\le D\frac{1-e^{-(\lambda+K)h}}{\lambda+K}
\le D\frac{1-e^{-\lambda h}}{\lambda}
\le Dh.
\]
Integrating along the interval connecting \(K,\widehat K\ge0\) gives
\[
\boxed{
|\phi_h(\widehat K)-\phi_h(K)|
\le D\frac{1-e^{-\lambda h}}{\lambda}
\left(\frac{\eta uC}{s\delta}E_\sigma+E_K^{\rm other}\right).
}
\]
If evaluating \(\phi_h\) using \(\widehat K\) has additional error bound \(E_{\rm eval}\) before final storage rounding, add at most \(E_{\rm eval}\) to this component's error relative to \(\phi_h(K)\). \(E_{\rm eval}\) includes evaluation errors from multiplication, division, exponentials, addition, subtraction, and other operations not yet counted; it does not recount the already included \(K\) error. This formula covers only the specified channel of component \(e\), not a full-vector error certificate. Direct changes to \(e,m,T,\lambda,h\) are accounted for separately. If the same-branch conditions fail, use the complete map in [Section 16.2](#sec-16-2) and the defect representation of [Appendix C.7](#sec-c-7).

**Error of the final stored value.** To avoid treating the channel bound above as the sum of all errors, retain identical \(e,m,T,\lambda,h\) and the same admissible-branch conditions, and define
\[
B_{\rm ch}:=D\frac{1-e^{-\lambda h}}{\lambda}E_K^{\rm tot},
\qquad B_{\rm pre}:=B_{\rm ch}+E_{\rm eval}.
\]
Let the value before final storage be
\(v_{\rm pre}=\phi_h(\widehat K)+\varepsilon_{\rm eval}\),
with \(|\varepsilon_{\rm eval}|\le E_{\rm eval}\), and the stored value be
\(\widehat e^+=R_{{\rm fp},e}(v_{\rm pre})\). On
\[
\mathcal I_{\rm pre}
=[\,\phi_h(K)-B_{\rm pre},\,\phi_h(K)+B_{\rm pre}\,]
\],
assume the same storage-rounding function is finitely defined throughout, and let
\[
E_{\rm store}:=
\sup_{v\in\mathcal I_{\rm pre}}
|R_{{\rm fp},e}(v)-v|<\infty.
\]
The triangle inequality gives
\[
\boxed{
|\widehat e^+-\phi_h(K)|
\le B_{\rm ch}+E_{\rm eval}+E_{\rm store}.
}
\]
Count every error source once. Sources already included in \(E_\sigma\) or \(E_K^{\rm other}\) must not be added again. With round-to-nearest and an admissible representational range, \(E_{\rm store}\) can be bounded by actual grid spacings covered by that interval; \(\operatorname{ulp}(1)/2\) is not a universal substitute. This supremum is a mathematical definition, not a supplied effective numerical bound for every hardware platform and operating point.

\(B_{\rm ch}\) bounds only the coefficient channel. Adding \(E_{\rm eval}\) covers prestorage error within this scope; adding \(E_{\rm store}\) then gives the poststorage bound. Even if \(B_{\rm ch}\) alone exceeds the observed error in one observation, that does not establish general coverage of omitted terms. When the stored value exactly equals start-of-step \(e\), its error relative to the target map equals \(|\phi_h(K)-e|\). This does not prove that arithmetic error or any individual channel equals that value.

**Accounting when the comparison reference changes.** Alternatively, use the exact map of all stored parameters and the stored step size,
\(\Phi_{\widehat h}(z;\widehat\pi)\),
as the coefficient-channel reference. If it satisfies this section's branch and domain conditions, denote its channel and evaluation/storage bounds by
\(B_{\rm ch}^{\rm st},E_{\rm eval}^{\rm st},E_{\rm store}^{\rm st}\). Comparison with the original target \(\Phi_h(z;\pi)\) then gives
\[
|\widehat e^+-\Phi_{h,e}(z;\pi)|
\le|\epsilon_{h,e}^{\rm rep}|
+B_{\rm ch}^{\rm st}+E_{\rm eval}^{\rm st}+E_{\rm store}^{\rm st}.
\]
The proof adds and subtracts
\(\Phi_{\widehat h,e}(z;\widehat\pi)\)
between the stored value and the original target, then applies this section's bound. Superscript \({\rm st}\) labels only the analysis reference, not a new emotion state.

Here \(B_{\rm ch}^{\rm st}\) no longer includes the representation difference from original to stored parameters. Conversely, if the original target remains the reference and threshold-representation error is already included in \(E_\sigma\), do not add that same error again. A computed channel difference is not automatically the defined channel upper bound \(B_{\rm ch}\). Both an actual difference and an upper bound must identify their comparison reference.

These comparisons concern real frozen maps at the same step-start state. Comparing complete continuous true solutions additionally requires frozen-approximation, initial-state, and other input errors under [Appendix C.7](#sec-c-7). If finite-precision intermediates select another branch or mode, the assumptions here must not conceal that difference.

\(\eta(e-m)\operatorname{ulp}(1)/\delta_r\) is not a general estimate of \(\epsilon_h^{\rm ar}\). It omits \(u,C,s\), error sources, branch conditions, and step size. \(\eta\) is a rate, so this form indicates at most a possible derivative-error scale. Only with fixed parameters, a threshold on the smooth active branch \(r>0\), and \(h\to0\) does
\[
\frac{\partial\phi_h}{\partial r}
=-h\,\frac{\eta(e-m)}s\,U_r+O(h^2),
\]
hold. The remainder constant depends on the fixed operating point and parameters; it is not asserted uniform in \(\delta_r\to0\). One-step error may exceed half the state-rounding-cell width, but whether it does requires actual signed error or valid bounds. This upper bound alone does not imply stagnation. The final condition in [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5) uses total error \(\epsilon^{\rm num}\) and actual spacings on both sides.

**Explicit small-step remainder bound.** Retain the same smooth active branch \(r>0\), differentiate only with respect to \(r\), and write
\[
d=e-m>0,\quad a=\lambda+K>0,\quad
V=\lambda(T-e)-Kd,\quad K_r=\frac{\eta}{s}U_r\ge0.
\]
Here \(d\) differs from the stepping coefficient \(d_j(h)\) in [Appendix C.2](#sec-c-2). For any finite \(h>0\),
\[
\boxed{
\left|\frac{\partial\phi_h}{\partial r}
+h\,dK_r\right|
\le\frac{K_r}{2}\bigl(ad+|V|\bigr)h^2.
}
\]
Proof: the frozen solution satisfies
\(x_K(v)-e=(V/a)(1-e^{-av})\), so
\(|x_K(v)-e|\le|V|v\). Subtracting \(-hd\) from the sensitivity integral gives
\[
\frac{\partial\phi_h}{\partial K}+hd
=d\int_0^h[1-e^{-a(h-v)}]\,dv
-\int_0^h e^{-a(h-v)}[x_K(v)-e]\,dv.
\]
Use \(1-e^{-at}\le at\), \(e^{-at}\le1\), and
\(\int_0^h v\,dv=h^2/2\), then multiply by \(K_r\) to obtain the result.

When \(K_r>0\), normalize by the nonzero leading-term magnitude \(h dK_r\) to obtain
\[
\frac{|\partial_r\phi_h+h dK_r|}{h dK_r}
\le\frac h2\left(a+\frac{|V|}{d}\right).
\]
This bounds the remainder relative to the leading term, not the same ratio relative to the exact derivative. If \(K_r=0\), both derivative and remainder are zero and no division is performed. Fixed-operating-point \(O(h^2)\) does not automatically give a relative-error constant depending only on \(ah\) and uniform over all admissible operating points; this bound still retains \(|V|/d\). Relative differences from finite samples do not remove these conditions.

Under time rescaling \(\tau'=c\tau\), divide \(a,V,K_r\) by \(c\), with \(h'=ch\). Both the absolute remainder bound and the relative bound above are unchanged. This is an analytical condition, not a new computational-step threshold or human emotion time constant.

**Finer finite-step bound and the second-order constant.** Retain all preceding conditions and define nonnegative functions for analysis only:
\[
\mathscr L_a(h)
:=\int_0^h[1-e^{-a(h-v)}]\,dv
=h-\frac{1-e^{-ah}}a,
\]
\[
\mathscr M_a(h)
:=\frac1a\int_0^h e^{-a(h-v)}(1-e^{-av})\,dv
=\frac{1-e^{-ah}-ah\,e^{-ah}}{a^2}.
\]
Substituting the same frozen solution yields the exact signed identity
\[
\boxed{
\partial_r\phi_h+h dK_r
=K_r\bigl[d\,\mathscr L_a(h)-V\,\mathscr M_a(h)\bigr].
}
\]
Hence
\[
|\partial_r\phi_h+h dK_r|
\le K_r\bigl[d\,\mathscr L_a(h)+|V|\,\mathscr M_a(h)\bigr]
\le\frac{K_r}{2}(ad+|V|)h^2.
\]
The first inequality only discards possible sign cancellation. From the integral definitions, for \(h>0,a>0\),
\[
0<\mathscr L_a(h)<ah^2/2,\qquad
0<\mathscr M_a(h)<h^2/2.
\]
Thus for every finite positive step satisfying \(K_r>0,d>0\), this bound retaining the exponentials is strictly smaller than the preceding second-order polynomial bound. When \(K_r=0\), both are zero. A claim that the bound cannot be tightened therefore requires a specified form and scope. Integral and closed forms are equivalent in real arithmetic. For very small \(ah\), the closed form still involves numerical cancellation and does not bypass the evaluation-error contract above.

On the other hand, in the original second-order form
\[
c\,K_r(ad+|V|)h^2
\],
if the same constant \(c\) must apply to every admissible operating point here and arbitrarily small \(h>0\), then \(c=1/2\) cannot be uniformly reduced. Proof: choose any admissible active equilibrium with \(V=0,K_r>0,d>0\), such as one guaranteed by the existence conditions of [Appendix D.9](#sec-d-9). The frozen trajectory then satisfies \(x_K(v)=e\), and
\[
\frac{|\partial_r\phi_h+h dK_r|}
{\tfrac12K_r(ad+|V|)h^2}
=\frac{2(ah-1+e^{-ah})}{(ah)^2}
\longrightarrow1\qquad(h\downarrow0).
\]
The limit follows by expanding \(e^{-t}=1-t+t^2/2+O(t^3)\) at \(t=0\). Thus any fixed \(c<1/2\) fails for sufficiently small positive steps. This proves optimality of the uniform constant in the specified second-order form, not equality at every operating point. It does not exclude the finer functional bound above or exact values exploiting sign cancellation.

This result is analytical and does not depend on a maximum ratio from external finite sampling. For time-rescaling factor \(\gamma>0\),
\(\mathscr L_{a/\gamma}(\gamma h)=\gamma\mathscr L_a(h)\) and
\(\mathscr M_{a/\gamma}(\gamma h)=\gamma^2\mathscr M_a(h)\).
Together with division of \(V,K_r\) by \(\gamma\), the remainder and finer bound still represent the same quantity. No psychological time, regulation mechanism, or parameter is added.

**Representation and scaling limits.** Increasing precision of subtraction and subsequent evaluation can reduce their arithmetic errors. An already approximated stored \(\theta\) does not recover its original rational value merely through higher-precision subtraction later. If the engineering value is specified by an exact numerator/denominator, retaining its source makes avoidance of premature rounding possible. The threshold-representation term in [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5) is zero only when the effective parameter actually retains the original value. Saving an additional fraction while continuing to use the same approximation does not remove the error. Nor does it eliminate later conversion, exponential, whole-step evaluation, or other parameter errors. High-precision decimal recomputation is still finite precision; without bounds enclosing the true value, it is not an exact-arithmetic proof. Cancellation and finite precision do not justify adding a psychological epsilon, rewriting thresholds, or stopping internal time.

If only the time unit changes by \(\tau'=c\tau,h'=ch\), then
\(\lambda'=\lambda/c,\eta'=\eta/c,K'=K/c\), while
\(r,s,C,u,\delta,E_\sigma,D\) is unchanged.
\[
\frac{1-e^{-\lambda'h'}}{\lambda'}=
c\frac{1-e^{-\lambda h}}{\lambda},\qquad
\frac{\eta'uC}{s\delta}=\frac1c\frac{\eta uC}{s\delta}.
\]
The consistently converted propagation bounds represent the same state error; errors in other rates transform with the same units. Different hardware, operation orders, and precision may follow different bitwise rounding paths, but do not thereby change human psychological time. This specification mandates no particular hardware or additional precision threshold.

<a id="sec-c-7"></a>

### C.7 Finite-Interval Error, Event Propagation, and Small Personality Changes

To distinguish first-order approximation, error estimation, and proved bounds, fix any finite abstract interval \([\tau_0,\tau_f]\). Let \(Z\) be the continuous-state vector participating in the analyzed dynamics for a fixed finite object set, using fixed time-independent coordinate scales and the same vector norm. The real solution and approximate reconstruction \(\widehat Z\) use identical valid inputs, event times, semantic order, and discrete-mode sequence. State-determined switches whose times or types change because of numerical error are not automatically covered.

The approximate reconstruction is absolutely continuous between events and remains in the region where the equations and Lipschitz bounds apply. Numerical grid points are not psychological events; use continuous piecewise reconstruction between them without adding emotional jumps at the grid. Assume
\[
\|\mathcal V(\tau,z_1)-\mathcal V(\tau,z_2)\|
\le\Lambda(\tau)\|z_1-z_2\|,\qquad
\Lambda\ge0,\quad \int_{\tau_0}^{\tau_f}\Lambda<\infty.
\]

For this proof only, compose same-time events into one map using their specified order. Distinct batch times
\(\tau_1<\cdots<\tau_N\) lie in \((\tau_0,\tau_f]\); the initial state is the value after the \(\tau_0\) batch commit. Let
\[
Z(\tau_k^+)=\mathcal J_k(Z(\tau_k^-)),\qquad
\|\mathcal J_k(z_1)-\mathcal J_k(z_2)\|
\le L_k^{\mathcal J}\|z_1-z_2\|,\quad
0\le L_k^{\mathcal J}<\infty.
\]
A composition bound may use the product of individual event Lipschitz bounds, without changing sequential appraisal and commitment semantics. [Appendix A.1](#sec-a-1) ensures well-definedness under the supplied contract. This section additionally requires explicit effective Lipschitz bounds; arbitrary language classifiers or discontinuous switches are not presumed compliant.

Define the continuous and event defects:
\[
\mathfrak r(\tau)=
\dot{\widehat Z}(\tau)-\mathcal V(\tau,\widehat Z(\tau)),\qquad
\mathfrak d_k=
\widehat Z(\tau_k^+)-
\mathcal J_k(\widehat Z(\tau_k^-)).
\]
Require integrable \(\mathfrak r\) and finite \(\mathfrak d_k\). These describe numerical-reconstruction error relative to the original equations; they do not enter the psychological dynamics. Let
\[
\mathcal W(\tau,s)=
\exp\!\left(\int_s^\tau\Lambda(v)\,dv\right)
\prod_{s<\tau_k\le\tau}L_k^{\mathcal J},\qquad s\le\tau.
\]
The post-event \(s=\tau_k^+\) excludes that event from the product; an empty product is 1. All observations use right-continuous states, and inter-event integrals ignore finitely many endpoints. Then
\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\|\widehat Z(\tau)-Z(\tau)\|
\le{}&\mathcal W(\tau,\tau_0)
\|\widehat Z(\tau_0)-Z(\tau_0)\|\\
&+\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau}
\mathcal W(\tau,s)\|\mathfrak r(s)\|\,ds\\
&+\sum_{\tau_k\le\tau}
\mathcal W(\tau,\tau_k^+)\|\mathfrak d_k\|.
\end{aligned}}
\]
Proof: between events, the error norm satisfies Gronwall's integral inequality with propagation factor
\(\exp(\int\Lambda)\). At events,
\(\varepsilon^+\le L_k^{\mathcal J}\varepsilon^-+\|\mathfrak d_k\|\).
Substitution in finite batch order yields the bound. No division by \(L_k^{\mathcal J}\) is required, so zero values are included.

For a fixed interval, fixed contract, and fixed bounded propagation factors, if the initial error, integral of the continuous defect, and sum of event defects are all \(O(h_{\max})\), this gives a uniform first-order global error bound. It does not guarantee the same error constant on increasingly long intervals, nor do nearby results at a few step sizes prove these defect bounds. Discretization, arithmetic rounding, integral-statistic, and input-encoding errors remain distinct. Under unit rescaling \(\tau'=c\tau\), divide \(\Lambda,\mathfrak r\) by \(c\); event bounds and defects remain unchanged, preserving the complete error relation.

**Affective-baseline error decomposition.** Along two compared paths, let
\[
R_E=r_E/H_b,\qquad
\dot b_i=R_E(t_i^E-b_i),
\]
\[
\dot{\widehat b}_i=
\widehat R_E(\widehat t_i^E-\widehat b_i)+\epsilon_{b,i}.
\]
Require \(R_E,\widehat R_E\ge0\), with both \(b\) and their targets remaining in the same
\(\mathcal B_i\). Approximate \(b\) must also have no event jumps; any jump error is handled separately by the event-defect terms above. When a path has \(Q_E=0\), it has \(R_E=0\), and its target may be chosen arbitrarily in \(\mathcal B_i\). The continuous equation still uses the nonsingular form in [Section 10.3](#sec-10-3). Let
\[
\Psi_E(\tau,s)=\exp\!\left(-\int_s^\tau R_E(v)\,dv\right).
\]
Since \(\mathcal B_i\) has width \(\rho_b\), the error satisfies
\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
|\widehat b_i(\tau)-b_i(\tau)|
\le{}&\Psi_E(\tau,\tau_0)|\widehat b_i(\tau_0)-b_i(\tau_0)|\\
&+\int_{\tau_0}^{\tau}\Psi_E(\tau,s)
\left[
R_E|\widehat t_i^E-t_i^E|
+\rho_b|\widehat R_E-R_E|
+|\epsilon_{b,i}|
\right](s)\,ds .
\end{aligned}}
\]
Proof: the error derivative equals
\[
-R_E(\widehat b_i-b_i)
+R_E(\widehat t_i^E-t_i^E)
+(\widehat R_E-R_E)(\widehat t_i^E-\widehat b_i)
+\epsilon_{b,i}.
\]
Apply the integrating factor and
\(|\widehat t_i^E-\widehat b_i|\le\rho_b\). This decomposition holds along complete closed-loop paths. Without bounds on target and integration-rate errors, it establishes no numerical personality-error percentage; their dependence on emotion states must not be ignored.

**Relative error for small values.** If a valid upper bound satisfies
\(|\widehat b_i-b_i|\le\varepsilon_i\) and
\(|\widehat b_i|>\varepsilon_i\), then
\[
\frac{|\widehat b_i-b_i|}{|b_i|}
\le\frac{\varepsilon_i}{|\widehat b_i|-\varepsilon_i}.
\]
If the bound includes zero, no finite relative guarantee of this form is available. Personality change additionally requires errors at both endpoints:
\[
|(\widehat b_i(\tau_f)-\widehat b_i(\tau_0))
-(b_i(\tau_f)-b_i(\tau_0))|
\le\varepsilon_i(\tau_f)+\varepsilon_i(\tau_0).
\]
Small changes may therefore have apparently large relative errors despite small absolute errors. A numerical-error percentage is not an annual personality-adaptation percentage or a human perceptual difference. Guarantees on significant digits and final output grid points require error intervals and quantization boundaries; they do not follow directly from being below one grid spacing or a few percentage points. The exact quantization contracts of [Section 15](#sec-15) and [Section 16.3.1](#sec-16-3-1) remain in force.

<a id="sec-c-8"></a>

### C.8 Time Averages, Interval-End Values, and Statistical Error

**Operational definitions of analytical quantities.** The following functions are for mathematical analysis only, not new psychological states or downstream output axes. On a finite abstract-time interval, use explicitly stated activity semantics to define nonoverlapping intervals
\(\mathcal G=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N_G}(\sigma_j,\upsilon_j)\),
\(\sigma_j<\upsilon_j\), with total length
\[
|\mathcal G|=\sum_j(\upsilon_j-\sigma_j)>0.
\]
Calling them gaps still requires stating which activities are excluded. No new messages does not automatically mean zero tasks, recall, or regulation.

Define the time-weighted fatigue average as
\[
\overline f_{\rm time}
=\frac1{|\mathcal G|}\int_{\mathcal G}f(\tau)\,d\tau.
\]
By contrast, for fixed nonnegative weights \(w_j^{\rm obs}\) with positive sum, the interval-end average is
\[
\overline f_{\rm end}
=\frac{\sum_j w_j^{\rm obs}f(\upsilon_j^-)}
{\sum_j w_j^{\rm obs}}.
\]
These generally differ. Uniform abstract-time sampling approximates the first expression only under the corresponding integration-approximation conditions. Many interval-end samples do not automatically constitute a time average. In particular, with unequal interval lengths, an unweighted sample average must not be assumed to average elapsed time.

If \(\mathcal G\) and the weights are fixed, and
\(|\widehat f(\tau)-f(\tau)|\le\varepsilon_f(\tau)\)
is already established, exact integration and endpoint evaluation of the reconstructed path give
\[
|\overline{\widehat f}_{\rm time}-\overline f_{\rm time}|
\le\frac1{|\mathcal G|}
\int_{\mathcal G}\varepsilon_f(\tau)\,d\tau,
\]
\[
|\overline{\widehat f}_{\rm end}-\overline f_{\rm end}|
\le
\frac{\sum_jw_j^{\rm obs}\varepsilon_f(\upsilon_j^-)}
{\sum_jw_j^{\rm obs}}.
\]
Additional numerical errors in integration or endpoint readings require their own bounds. If interval selection also depends on the numerical state, error from changes in the selected set must not be omitted. With no eligible intervals, these averages are undefined, not zero.

Statistics with different sampling conditions, weights, or denominators cannot directly be compared as the same emotion indicator. Pure time-unit conversion multiplies both integral and total interval length by the same factor, leaving the first expression unchanged. The second is also unchanged when weights and corresponding endpoints are retained. Neither definition introduces actual dates, fixed conversation-turn durations, or calibrated human timescales.

<a id="sec-d"></a>

## Appendix D Closed-Loop Fatigue and Regulation Analysis

<a id="sec-d-1"></a>

### D.1 Analyzable Strength of Fatigue Effects

With fixed \(e,m,R,p^{\rm reg},g^{\rm keep},\chi\) and parameters, the automatic policy gives:
\[
K_i(f)=
\frac{\eta_i R u_{\max}^{\rm reg}r_i^{\rm reg}}
{\delta_r s_f^{1-\chi}+s_f S_r}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\frac{dK_i}{df}
=-\frac{\eta_i R u_{\max}^{\rm reg}r_i^{\rm reg}\rho_f
[\delta_r(1-\chi)s_f^{-\chi}+S_r]}
{[\delta_r s_f^{1-\chi}+s_f S_r]^2}\le0.
\]
Also,
\[
\sum_i u_{{\rm reg},i}^{\rm auto}
=R u_{\max}^{\rm reg}\frac{s_f^\chi S_r}{\delta_r+s_f^\chi S_r}\le R,
\]
\[
\frac d{df}\sum_i u_{{\rm reg},i}^{\rm auto}
=\frac{R u_{\max}^{\rm reg}\delta_r S_r\chi\rho_f s_f^{\chi-1}}
{(\delta_r+s_f^\chi S_r)^2}\ge0.
\]
Thus, for fixed current demand, compensation increases or maintains effort but does not make fatigue increase the effective down-regulation coefficient. This is a local conditional result. \(e,m,R\), goals, and attention change over a complete history, so it does not imply monotonicity of all peaks, areas, or durations.

For fixed explicit effort \(u>0\), fixed background, \(T=m\), \(e>m\), and fixed \(D^{\rm att}\), the local recovery time is
\[
t_{\rm loc}(f)=[\Lambda_i+\eta_i u/(1+\rho_ff)]^{-1},
\quad \Lambda_i=\lambda_{e,i}+D_i^{\rm att}.
\]
Comparing \(f_H>f_L\), let \(A_L=(1+\rho_ff_L)^{-1}\) and \(A_H=(1+\rho_ff_H)^{-1}\). Then
\[
R_{\rm loc}=\frac{\Lambda_i+\eta_i u A_L}{\Lambda_i+\eta_i u A_H},\qquad
\eta_i=\frac{(R_{\rm loc}-1)\Lambda_i}{u(A_L-R_{\rm loc}A_H)}
\]
A positive finite coefficient is obtained only when \(1<R_{\rm loc}<A_L/A_H\). The automatic policy requires substituting \(K_i(f)\) above; the fixed-\(u\) expression is not a whole-model ratio.

Whenever \(\rho_f,\eta_i,R,u_{\max}^{\rm reg},r_i^{\rm reg}>0\), fatigue has a nonzero default effect through regulation. This pathway may be zero when demand is below threshold, emotion is retained, no spare effort capacity exists, or processing is fully suspended. Fatigue output \(y_{\rm Fat}\) still retains current fatigue and does not disappear without a down-regulation target. Changes below three-decimal resolution remain internal.

Use \(\rho_f,\eta_i\) as specified. Fatigue does not directly multiply natural decay rates or add to insult/loss intensity.

<a id="sec-d-2"></a>

### D.2 Fatigue Equilibrium with Automatic Compensation and Fixed Demand

**Mathematical result under the following conditions only.** Let \(u_{\rm task}=u_{\rm rec}=0\), with the sum \(S=S_r>0\) of \(r_i^{\rm reg}\) fixed; fix \(\chi,\rho_f,q=q_{\rm rest}\), \(\omega=\omega_{\rm reg}\), \(\delta=\delta_r\), and \(u=u_{\max}^{\rm reg}\). Explicitly require \(a_f,b_f,\delta_r>0\), \(0<u\le1\), \(0<q,\omega\le1\), \(\rho_f\ge0\), and \(0\le\chi\le1\), with all parameters finite and \(0<S\le|\mathcal N|=6\). Use automatic regulation rather than an explicit effort override. Define
\[
C(f)=(1+\rho_ff)^\chi,\qquad
U(f)=\frac{uC(f)S}{\delta+C(f)S}.
\]
Then \(\mathcal L=\omega U,\ \mathcal R_f=q(1-U)\), so
\[
\dot f=
\frac{a_f\omega uC(f)S(1-f)
-b_fq[\delta+(1-u)C(f)S]f}
{\delta+C(f)S}.
\]
The right-hand side is positive at \(f=0\) and negative at \(f=1\). Interior equilibria satisfy
\[
\boxed{
H(f^*)=\frac{a_f\omega}{b_fq}uS,\qquad
H(f)=\frac{f}{1-f}\left[\frac{\delta}{(1+\rho_ff)^\chi}+(1-u)S\right].
}
\]
For \(0<f<1\),
\[
\frac d{df}\log\frac{f}{(1-f)(1+\rho_ff)^\chi}
=\frac1{f(1-f)}-\frac{\chi\rho_f}{1+\rho_ff}>0,
\]
because \(0\le\chi\le1\) and
\(1/[f(1-f)]>1/f>\rho_f/(1+\rho_ff)\).
Thus \(H\) is strictly increasing, with \(H(0)=0,\ H(1^-)=\infty\), giving a unique \(f^*\in(0,1)\).

Furthermore,
\[
\dot f=\frac{b_fq\,C(f)(1-f)}{\delta+C(f)S}
\left[\frac{a_f\omega}{b_fq}uS-H(f)\right].
\]
Hence the state increases when \(f<f^*\) and decreases when \(f>f^*\); every initial value in \([0,1]\) converges to \(f^*\). This proves global asymptotic stability of the fixed-demand scalar subsystem, not global stability of the entire emotion feedback loop.

Degenerate cases: if \(S=0\) or \(u=0\), then \(U=0\). If \(q>0\), then \(f\to0\); otherwise \(f\) remains constant. When \(q>0,\omega=0\), \(f\to0\) also holds because \(1-U\) has a positive lower bound. If \(q=0,\omega>0,S,u>0\), then \(f\to1\). If both accumulation and recovery terms vanish, \(f\) remains constant.

In the complete model, \(S_r\) generally depends on \(e,m\) and regulation goals; fixing it does not retain that feedback. In particular, long-term equilibrium gives only
\[
e_i-m_i=(1-\kappa)(e_i-b_i),
\]
and does not guarantee that \(m_i\) eventually equals \(e_i\) under continuous sources, or that regulatory deviation ultimately vanishes.

To solve only this conditional equilibrium, use the original fatigue right-hand side before division by \(1-f\) and find a root between the opposite-sign endpoints of \([0,1]\). Monotonicity of \(H\) guarantees uniqueness. This avoids directly evaluating infinite \(H(1)\). Root-solver iterations are not psychological time.

<a id="sec-d-3"></a>

### D.3 Fatigue Intensity, Recovery, and Parameter Distinguishability

For any current \(\mathcal L,\mathcal R_f\), if
\(k_f=a_f\mathcal L+b_f\mathcal R_f>0\), then
\[
\dot f=k_f(f_{\rm inst}^*-f),\qquad
f_{\rm inst}^*=\frac{a_f\mathcal L}{a_f\mathcal L+b_f\mathcal R_f}.
\]
\(f_{\rm inst}^*\) is the current fixed-coefficient target, not automatically a closed-loop equilibrium. Even when \(\mathcal L>0\), fatigue decreases if \(f>f_{\rm inst}^*\). Recovery only during rest is therefore not a property of this model. Without recovery opportunity, a nonzero load cannot decrease fatigue.

This section further specifies parameter relationships without prescribing a human target fatigue level. Retain the conditions of [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2), and let \(C_{\max}=(1+\rho_f)^\chi\):
\[
U_-=\frac{uS}{\delta+S},\qquad
U_+=\frac{uC_{\max}S}{\delta+C_{\max}S},
\]
\[
\Psi(U)=\frac{a_f\omega U}{a_f\omega U+b_fq(1-U)}.
\]
Since \(U_-\le U(f)\le U_+\) and \(\Psi\) is increasing, the unique equilibrium satisfies
\[
\boxed{\Psi(U_-)\le f^*\le\Psi(U_+).}
\]
Approximating \(C_f\) by 1 gives a lower bound under fixed demand; it equals the actual \(C_f\) only when \(\chi\rho_f=0\). An expansion assuming small \(f\) that yields moderate or high \(f^*\) cannot still be called the exact equilibrium solution.

If independent evidence later specifies an admissible equilibrium \(f^\dagger\in(0,1)\), the required effort must satisfy
\[
U^\dagger
=\frac{b_fq f^\dagger}
{a_f\omega(1-f^\dagger)+b_fq f^\dagger}.
\]
Policy compatibility additionally requires \(0<U^\dagger<u\), with
\[
\boxed{\delta_r=S(1+\rho_ff^\dagger)^\chi
\left(\frac{u}{U^\dagger}-1\right)>0.}
\]
This is a parameter-compatibility relation, not an instruction to retune parameters to a chosen scenario. This specification selects no human target \(f^\dagger\). If the conditions fail, \(\delta_r\) must not be forced negative, nor can changing only one coefficient be claimed to preserve every dynamical property.

Increasing \(\delta_r\) reduces applied regulation, its fatigue cost, and the recovery capacity it occupies simultaneously; it changes the emotional time course itself. Decreasing \(\omega_{\rm reg}\) only lowers the weight of fatigue accumulation, while retaining the recovery-capacity occupancy of actual effort. A single \(f^*\) cannot uniquely distinguish the effects of \(\delta_r,\omega_{\rm reg},a_f/b_f,q_{\rm rest}\), so one fatigue equilibrium cannot validate every parameter.

Finally, the event maps contain no direct fatigue gain. For fixed pre-event \(e,m,w,s\), encoded appraisal, mask, and dose,
\[
\frac{\partial e_i^+}{\partial f^-}
=\frac{\partial w_i^+}{\partial f^-}=0.
\]
because the common and reappraisal flows in [Section 11](#sec-11) do not contain \(f\). This partial derivative excludes historical effects of prior fatigue on the pre-event state. Fatigue may change post-event regulation and continuous evolution, but increased emotional area does not mean increased instantaneous stimulus gain has been implemented, much less that greater human sensitivity has been established universally.

<a id="sec-d-4"></a>

### D.4 Conditional Characterization of Closed-Loop Psychological Equilibria

First define equilibrium semantics. Consider an interval without new events and with constant mode and external inputs. Every psychological state included in the analysis and every internal state feeding back into the vector field is constant in time. Purely accounting, nonfeedback quantities \(\Xi_E,D_E\) may continue increasing at their rates; psychological equilibrium does not require accounting to stop. A finite replay budget being consumed still affects future sources and must be included, or that activity excluded. A temporary plateau is not a complete equilibrium.

Let
\[
\vartheta_f=(a_f,b_f,\omega_{\rm reg},q_{\rm rest},
u_{\max}^{\rm reg},\delta_r,\rho_f,\chi).
\]
Within the nondegenerate parameter domain of [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2), define
\[
\mathscr F_f(S;\vartheta_f)
=\text{unique fatigue root at fixed demand }S>0\text{},
\qquad \mathscr F_f(0;\vartheta_f)=0.
\]
This is an algebraic function defined by the equations, not a new psychological state or evolution law.

**Proposition.** Suppose a closed-loop psychological equilibrium \(Z_*\) exists, uses automatic regulation, and satisfies \(u_{\rm task}=u_{\rm rec}=0\), with parameters satisfying the nondegenerate conditions of [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2). Substituting
\[
S_*=\sum_{i\in\mathcal N}
p_i^{\rm reg}(1-g_i^{\rm keep})
[e_{*,i}-m_{*,i}-\theta_i^r]_+
\]
and the equilibrium value of \(\vartheta_{f,*}\) necessarily gives
\[
\boxed{f_*=\mathscr F_f(S_*;\vartheta_{f,*}).}
\]
If \(S_*=0\), then \(q_{\rm rest}>0\) implies \(f_*=0\). If goals or recovery quality depend on psychological state, use their equilibrium values here; they must not be presumed identical at other states.

Proof: psychological equilibrium requires \(\dot f=0\). Substituting equilibrium effort, recovery, and demand yields exactly the scalar equation in [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2). Its nondegenerate root is unique, proving the result. Conversely, satisfying this equality with the other quantities given makes only the fatigue equation zero; it does not make all other state equations zero.

This is a necessary condition for closed-loop equilibrium and an exact characterization of the fatigue equation once other equilibrium quantities are supplied. It proves neither existence nor uniqueness of \(S_*\) or the whole equilibrium, nor its stability. Different closed-loop equilibria may have different \(S_*\) while each satisfies the same root relation. Nonzero task/recall effort, explicit effort overrides, or degenerate parameters require the complete equations or corresponding degenerate branches, not this proposition.

<a id="sec-d-5"></a>

### D.5 Inverse Parameter Problems and Closed-Loop Feedback

The inverse expression for \(\delta_r\) in [Appendix D.3](#sec-d-3) is exact for fixed demand, not an independent tuning formula for the complete feedback loop. Let \(S>0\), retain the other conditions of [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2), and write
\[
\mathcal G(f,S,\delta_r)=
\frac{f}{1-f}
\left[\frac{\delta_r}{C(f)}+(1-u)S\right]
-\frac{a_f\omega}{b_fq}uS.
\]
Along its unique root,
\[
\partial_f\mathcal G>0,\qquad
\partial_{\delta_r}\mathcal G=\frac{f}{(1-f)C(f)}>0,
\]
\[
\left.\partial_S\mathcal G\right|_{\mathcal G=0}
=-\frac{f\delta_r}{(1-f)C(f)S}<0.
\]
The conditional root function is therefore differentiable, with
\[
\boxed{\partial_{\delta_r}\mathscr F_f<0,\qquad
\partial_S\mathscr F_f>0.}
\]
Thus increasing \(\delta_r\) lowers the fixed-demand fatigue root, but closed-loop demand need not remain fixed.

If old demand \(S_{\rm old}\) is used to invert for a new \(\delta_r\), yielding
\(\mathscr F_f(S_{\rm old};\vartheta_{\rm new})=f^\dagger\), while the new closed-loop equilibrium satisfies \(S_{\rm new}>S_{\rm old}\), then
\[
f_{\rm new}
=\mathscr F_f(S_{\rm new};\vartheta_{\rm new})>f^\dagger.
\]
If demand instead falls, the bias direction may reverse. If demand remains identical, the inverse remains exact for the fatigue equilibrium. Both roots must satisfy the stated conditions; one history cannot establish that the inverse always underestimates closed-loop fatigue.

**Definition of the complete inverse problem.** Fix admissible constant external inputs \(U_*\) and historical quantities that truly no longer evolve. Let \(z\) contain the remaining feedback states, \(\theta\) the parameters under consideration, and \(\mathcal V(z;\theta,U_*)\) the corresponding continuous vector field. Still-learning \(b,s\) and still-consumed budgets cannot arbitrarily be held constant. Define
\[
\mathcal E_\theta
=\{z\in\mathcal D:
\mathcal V(z;\theta,U_*)=0\},
\],
where \(\mathcal D\) contains every state-domain and spatial constraint. If independent evidence later supplies \(f^\dagger\), compatible parameters and equilibria must lie in
\[
\boxed{\{(\theta,z):
\theta\text{ satisfies all parameter-domain constraints},\
z\in\mathcal E_\theta,\
f(z)=f^\dagger\}.}
\]
This set may be empty, a singleton, or have multiple elements. It defines a mathematical inverse problem, not a calibration/validation procedure. Without conditions on closed-loop equilibrium uniqueness, stability, and parameter monotonicity, a one-dimensional parameter search need not produce a unique, reachable, or stable solution.

For local conditions, first fix truly conserved/frozen quantities and select independent state coordinates to solve for. Suppose the equilibrium lies in the interior of their admissible domain, the varied parameter has an open neighborhood within its admissible domain, and same-mode \(\mathcal V\) is \(C^1\) on the corresponding open neighborhood. If
\[
J=\partial_z\mathcal V(z_*;\theta_*,U_*)
\]
is invertible, the implicit function theorem gives a locally unique differentiable equilibrium branch, with
\[
\frac{dz_*}{d\theta}
=-J^{-1}\partial_\theta\mathcal V.
\]
This is local sensitivity obtained by differentiating equilibrium equations, not stability or global uniqueness of the branch. At a state-domain boundary, even a smooth extension and invertible Jacobian require a separate proof that the branch remains admissible; out-of-domain roots are not psychological equilibria. The formula does not apply directly at clip/positive-part switching points, discrete-mode changes, or singular \(J\).

In particular, along a smooth equilibrium branch varying only \(\theta=\delta_r\), with other \(\vartheta_f\) fixed,
\[
\frac{df_*}{d\delta_r}
=\partial_{\delta_r}\mathscr F_f
+\partial_S\mathscr F_f\,\frac{dS_*}{d\delta_r}.
\]
The second term is the feedback omitted by the fixed-demand inverse; its sign and size cannot be erased in advance. Changes in \(\omega_{\rm reg}\) may also affect emotion through \(f\to C_f,K\to e,m\to S\). A small effect in one numerical result is not a general theorem. No actual parameter estimates are added here.

Along the smooth branch for \(S_*>0\) above, define a dimensionless feedback ratio
\[
\Gamma_\delta
=-\frac{\partial_S\mathscr F_f\,(dS_*/d\delta_r)}
        {\partial_{\delta_r}\mathscr F_f},
\qquad
\frac{df_*}{d\delta_r}
=\partial_{\delta_r}\mathscr F_f(1-\Gamma_\delta).
\]
Its denominator is strictly negative, so it is well-defined. If \(dS_*/d\delta_r>0\), then \(\Gamma_\delta>0\), and demand feedback offsets the direct decreasing effect. At \(\Gamma_\delta=1\), the first-order total effect is zero; at \(\Gamma_\delta>1\), its direction reverses. If demand falls, \(\Gamma_\delta<0\), and feedback strengthens the direct effect. For a general complete feedback loop, this specification does not prove that the ratio is small, lies between 0 and 1, or is identical across operating points. [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8) gives a conditional result for a fixed-source single-axis branch. This is a local derivative decomposition, not a percentage attribution of fatigue's causes.

Only if every eigenvalue of the reduced-state Jacobian has strictly negative real part in the same admissible smooth neighborhood does the linearization theorem give local asymptotic stability of that equilibrium in the stated reduced dynamics. Omitted feedback states preclude claiming complete-system local stability from this result. Jacobian invertibility alone is insufficient. Numerical eigenvalues at one operating point represent neither other parameters nor a global stability proof.

<a id="sec-d-6"></a>

### D.6 Feedback Reversal and an Unstable Equilibrium in the Admissible Domain

**Mathematical result: analytical study of the model equations.** This section proves that \(\Gamma_\delta>1\) is more than an unexcluded logical possibility. The following algebraic counterexample uses nondefault parameters of the specified equations. It is not human data, psychological advice, a conversational scenario, or a simulation procedure.

**Restricted branch and Jacobian.** Fix one \(i\in\mathcal N\) and omit its axis subscript. Fix \(b\), continuous target \(A\), and \(\alpha=1\), and use automatic regulation with
\[
u_{\rm task}=u_{\rm rec}=0,\quad
p^{\rm reg}=1,\quad g^{\rm keep}=0,\quad
\chi=0,\quad u_{\max}^{\rm reg}=q_{\rm rest}=\omega_{\rm reg}=1.
\]
All other axes remain at their source-free zero states. Set \(Q_E=P^E=j_E=0\) with no experience registration, so the affective baseline is genuinely fixed. All other feedback quantities are fixed at admissible equilibria or supplied as fixed inputs. Set
\[
S=e-m-\theta^r>0,\quad U=\frac{S}{\delta_r+S},\quad
s_f=1+\rho_f f.
\]
On \(e<A\) and a smooth mood-projection branch, the attentional-disengagement positive part vanishes. The three coupled states satisfy
\[
\dot e=\lambda_e(A-e)-\frac{\eta U}{s_f}(e-m),\quad
\dot m=\lambda_m[(1-\kappa)b+\kappa e-m],
\]
\[
\dot f=a_fU(1-f)-b_f(1-U)f.
\]
\(w\) is a stable filter driven by \(e,m\) and does not feed back into these three equations. At any such equilibrium, let
\[
U_S=\frac{\delta_r}{(\delta_r+S)^2},\quad
h=\frac{\eta}{s_f}[U+(e-m)U_S],\quad
g=\frac{\eta\rho_fU(e-m)}{s_f^2},
\]
\[
r=[a_f(1-f)+b_ff]U_S,\qquad
\nu=a_fU+b_f(1-U).
\]
Here \(h,g,r,\nu\) are local Jacobian coefficients only; \(g\) is not the temperament gain \(g_i\) of [Section 7](#sec-7). Direct differentiation gives
\[
J_3=
\begin{pmatrix}
-\lambda_e-h&h&g\\
\lambda_m\kappa&-\lambda_m&0\\
r&-r&-\nu
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its characteristic polynomial is \(z^3+a_1z^2+a_2z+a_3\), where
\[
a_1=\lambda_e+h+\lambda_m+\nu>0,
\]
\[
a_2=\lambda_m[\lambda_e+h(1-\kappa)]
+\nu(\lambda_e+h+\lambda_m)-gr,
\]
\[
a_3=\lambda_m\{\nu[\lambda_e+h(1-\kappa)]
-gr(1-\kappa)\}.
\]
The Routh–Hurwitz conditions for a real cubic give
\[
\boxed{J_3\text{ is Hurwitz}
\iff a_2>0,\ a_3>0,\ a_1a_2>a_3.}
\]
This is verified by positivity of the first column of the Routh table,
\(1,a_1,(a_1a_2-a_3)/a_1,a_3\). Including \(w\) adds only eigenvalue \(-\lambda_x<0\). Satisfaction of the inequalities is therefore sufficient for local exponential stability of this smooth reduced dynamics. When they fail, positive and zero real parts must be distinguished; nonlinear instability cannot be declared in every case. These are not stability conditions for every input of the complete model, nor new globally prohibited parameter regions.

**Admissible analytical counterexample.** Take
\[
b=0,\ A=\tfrac12,\quad
\kappa=\tfrac12,\quad \ell=\tfrac14,\quad
\lambda_e=1,\ \lambda_m=\tfrac1{200},\ \lambda_x=\tfrac14,
\]
\[
k^{\rm reg}=10,\quad\eta=k^{\rm reg}\lambda_e=10,\quad
\rho_f=100,\quad
a_f=\tfrac1{100},\quad b_f=1,\quad
\delta_r=\tfrac1{100000},\quad
\theta^r=\frac{5151}{22604}-\frac1{1000}.
\]
Unlisted general parameters may use the initial values of [Section 12.3](#sec-12-3), retaining the fixed activity and source conditions above. All listed parameters lie in the domain of [Section 12.2](#sec-12-2); \(\theta^r\in(0,1)\) and the timescale-separation relations also hold. These numbers establish existence only; they are not new engineering defaults.

Let
\[
e_*=\frac{5151}{11302},\quad
m_*=\frac{5151}{22604},\quad
w_*=\frac{12019}{22604},\quad f_*=\frac12.
\]
Then
\[
S_*=\frac1{1000},\quad U_*=\frac{100}{101},\quad
s_{f,*}=51,\quad K_*=\frac{1000}{5151}.
\]
From \(m_*=e_*/2\) and \(w_*=7e_*/6\), obtain \(x_*=e_*\), with \(x_*\) strictly inside the constraint box. Since \(e_*<A\) and \(S_*>0\), this is indeed a smooth branch. Also,
\[
\lambda_e(A-e_*)=K_*(e_*-m_*),\qquad
a_fU_*(1-f_*)=b_f(1-U_*)f_*.
\]
All four state equations are therefore zero, and all psychological states and work fractions are admissible.

Substitution into the Jacobian coefficients gives exactly
\[
\boxed{a_3=-\frac{92438733}{97997946700}<0.}
\]
The characteristic polynomial is negative at zero and tends to positive infinity on the positive real axis, so it has at least one positive real eigenvalue. This equilibrium is unstable within an admissible invariant subspace, disproving the claim that every admissible equilibrium of the complete model is stable. Boundedness, solution uniqueness, and spatial constraints remain valid because none requires every equilibrium to attract.

**\(\Gamma_\delta>1\) at the same point.** Fix the parameters above and vary only \(\delta_r\) along the equilibrium branch, holding threshold \(\theta^r\) fixed. Let \(\beta=a_f/b_f=1/100\). The equilibrium fatigue equation gives
\[
U(f)=\frac{f}{\beta+(1-\beta)f}.
\]
Then, from emotion and background equilibrium, let \(d(f)=e-m\) to obtain
\[
d(f)=\frac{1/2}{2+10U(f)/(1+100f)},\quad
S(f)=d(f)-\theta^r,\quad
\delta_r(f)=\beta S(f)\frac{1-f}{f}.
\]
Exact differentiation at \(f=1/2\) gives
\[
d'(1/2)=\frac{1237000}{31933801},\qquad
\delta_r'(1/2)=\frac{277316199}{798345025000}>0.
\]
A smooth equilibrium branch therefore exists near this point, with \(df_*/d\delta_r>0\). The fixed-demand root simplifies here to
\[
\mathscr F_f(S;\delta_r)=\frac{\beta S}{\beta S+\delta_r},
\quad
\left.\partial_{\delta_r}\mathscr F_f\right|_*=-25000.
\]
By the definition in [Appendix D.5](#sec-d-5),
\[
\boxed{
\Gamma_\delta
=1+\frac{1}{25000\,\delta_r'(1/2)}
=\frac{309250000}{277316199}>1.
}
\]
This is a directional reversal along an equilibrium branch, not a guarantee of a stable reachable response. The equilibrium has already been proved unstable. This unstable branch must not be used as a human-fatigue prediction or a reachable target for inverse parameter selection.

Under time rescaling, divide \(J_3\) by the scale factor and \(a_j\) by its \(j\)th power. The Hurwitz inequalities and instability result remain unchanged, as does \(\Gamma_\delta\). The complete model still requires calibration against human data. This section does not establish instability of the default branch in [Section 12.3](#sec-12-3).

The ratio in this counterexample may also be written as
\[
\Gamma_\delta=\frac{309250000}{277316199}>1,
\]

<a id="sec-d-7"></a>

### D.7 Conditional Permutation Equivariance of Negative Axes

Fix an interval without new events, and let \(\Pi\) be a permutation matrix exchanging only axes within \(\mathcal N\) while preserving the others. Suppose two instances' per-axis parameters, initial values, mapped continuous targets, experience profiles, masks, cause-disengagement data, and regulation targets correspond under the same \(\Pi\), with all scalar activity and non-axis-specific inputs identical. Then the continuous vector field satisfies
\[
\mathcal V_{\rm emo}(\Pi Z;\Pi U)
=\Pi\mathcal V_{\rm emo}(Z;U),\qquad
\mathcal V_f(\Pi Z;\Pi U)=\mathcal V_f(Z;U).
\]
Here \(\Pi Z\) acts simultaneously on per-axis quantities such as \(b,m,e,w,P^E\), preserving \(f,Q_E,\Xi_E,D_E\) and object dispositions. State-dependent input rules must have the same equivariance; otherwise the proposition does not apply.

Proof: per-axis convex updates, positive parts, and clip preserve their form under consistent permutation. \(S_r\) and \(\sum_i u_{{\rm reg},i}\) are invariant under permutations of negative axes, leaving total compensatory effort, load, and recovery unchanged. Solution uniqueness therefore makes corresponding emotion trajectories permutations of one another and gives identical fatigue trajectories.

The engineering initial values for negative axes in [Section 12.3](#sec-12-3) have several such symmetries, but the underlying appraisal constructs and functions differ. Exchanging verbal names is not exchanging mapped numerical inputs. Different negative sources producing regulation fatigue is a computational capability of the model; similar numbers alone do not establish equal human fatigue costs across negative emotions. This proposition does not change A as anger or F as fear/anxiety.

<a id="sec-d-8"></a>

### D.8 Single-Axis Closed-Loop Sensitivity with Compensatory Effort

This section analyzes a single-axis feedback loop, including the direct effort dependence on fatigue when \(\chi>0\), nondegeneracy conditions, and stability scope.

**Conditions and notation.** Activate only one negative axis, using automatic regulation and \(p^{\rm reg}=1,\ g^{\rm keep}=0,\ u_{\rm task}=u_{\rm rec}=0\). There are no new events or experience registrations, and \(Q_E=P^E=j_E=0\). Fix \(b\), mapped source \(A\), attention intensity \(\alpha\), goals, and all other feedback quantities. In addition to [Section 12.2](#sec-12-2), require
\[
0<\alpha\le1,\quad b<A\le1,\quad 0<\kappa<1,\quad
\eta>0,\quad
0<u:=u_{\max}^{\rm reg}\le1,\quad
q:=q_{\rm rest}\in(0,1],\quad\omega:=\omega_{\rm reg}\in(0,1].
\]
while allowing
\(\rho_f\ge0,\ \chi\in[0,1]\), with all parameters finite, and
\(\lambda:=\lambda_e,\lambda_m,a_f,b_f,\delta:=\delta_r>0\).
\(A\) is the mapped scalar target, not an additional designation of the anger axis. Let
\[
T=(1-\alpha)m+\alpha A,\quad
d=e-m,\quad S=d-\theta^r>0,\quad e<T,
\]
\[
s_f=1+\rho_ff,\quad C=s_f^\chi,\quad
U=\frac{uCS}{\delta+CS}.
\]
When \(\alpha<1\), \(e<A\) alone does not make the attentional-disengagement term zero; \(e<T\) is required. At an active equilibrium in this section, \(\lambda(T-e)=\eta Ud/s_f>0\), so that condition holds automatically and a neighborhood on the same branch exists. Actual degenerate endpoints and switching points use the complete original equations, not these derivatives.

The three-dimensional vector field is
\[
\mathcal V=
\begin{pmatrix}
\lambda[(1-\alpha)m+\alpha A-e]-\eta Ud/s_f\\
\lambda_m[(1-\kappa)b+\kappa e-m]\\
a_f\omega U(1-f)-b_fq(1-U)f
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
\(w\) does not feed back into this subsystem. Sentiments and source appraisals that continue changing fall outside the fixed-source assumptions here.

**Complete partial derivatives.** Holding the other independent variables fixed,
\[
U_S=\frac{uC\delta}{(\delta+CS)^2}>0,\quad
U_f=\frac{u\delta S C'(f)}{(\delta+CS)^2}\ge0,\quad
U_\delta=-\frac{uCS}{(\delta+CS)^2}<0.
\]
Define local coefficients
\[
B=a_f\omega(1-f)+b_fqf,\qquad
k_f=a_f\omega U+b_fq(1-U),
\]
\[
h=\frac{\eta}{s_f}(U+dU_S),\quad
g=\frac{\eta d}{s_f}\left(\frac{\rho_fU}{s_f}-U_f\right),\quad
r=BU_S,\quad \nu=k_f-BU_f.
\]
Here \(h\) is a Jacobian coefficient, not the computational step size of [Section 16](#sec-16); \(g\) is not a temperament gain either. When \(\chi>0\), it must not still be replaced by \(g,\nu\) from [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6).

From
\[
\frac{U_f}{U}=
\frac{\chi\rho_f}{s_f}\frac{\delta}{\delta+CS}
\le\frac{\rho_f}{s_f}
\],
obtain \(g\ge0\). At fatigue equilibrium, \(0<U<u\le1,\ 0<f<1\), and
\[
\frac{BU_f}{k_f}
=\frac{f(1-f)U_f}{U(1-U)}
=\frac{f(1-f)\chi\rho_f}{s_f}
\frac{1-U/u}{1-U}<1,
\]
Hence \(\nu>0\), consistent with strict attraction of the fixed-demand root in [Appendix D.2](#sec-d-2).

Let
\[
D_\alpha=1-\kappa(1-\alpha)>0,\qquad
L=\lambda D_\alpha+h(1-\kappa)>0.
\]
Direct differentiation and expansion of the characteristic polynomial give
\[
J_3=
\begin{pmatrix}
-\lambda-h&h+\lambda(1-\alpha)&g\\
\lambda_m\kappa&-\lambda_m&0\\
r&-r&-\nu
\end{pmatrix},
\]
\[
a_1=\lambda+h+\lambda_m+\nu>0,
\]
\[
a_2=\lambda_m L+\nu(\lambda+h+\lambda_m)-gr,\qquad
a_3=\lambda_m[\nu L-gr(1-\kappa)].
\]
The cubic Hurwitz criterion in [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6) still applies to these coefficients.

**Proposition: directional reversal and determinant sign.** On a local branch of admissible smooth equilibria with \(a_3\ne0\),
\[
\boxed{\Gamma_\delta>1\iff a_3<0,\qquad
\Gamma_\delta<1\iff a_3>0.}
\]
Here \(\Gamma_\delta\) is defined along the complete three-dimensional equilibrium branch with all other parameters fixed. Proof: let
\[
c_1=\frac{\eta d}{s_f}(-U_\delta)>0,\qquad
c_2=B(-U_\delta)>0,\qquad
\partial_\delta\mathcal V=(c_1,0,-c_2)^\top.
\]
The cofactors required for the third row of the adjugate are
\[
C_{13}=\lambda_mr(1-\kappa),\qquad C_{33}=\lambda_mL.
\]
Thus
\[
\mathscr N_\delta:=
[\operatorname{adj}(J_3)\partial_\delta\mathcal V]_3
=-\lambda_m(-U_\delta)B
\left[\lambda D_\alpha+(1-\kappa)\frac{\eta U}{s_f}\right]<0.
\]
Using \(\det J_3=-a_3\) and the implicit function theorem,
\[
\boxed{\frac{df_*}{d\delta}=\frac{\mathscr N_\delta}{a_3}.}
\]
Combine this with
\(df_*/d\delta=(\partial_\delta\mathscr F_f)(1-\Gamma_\delta)\) and
\(\partial_\delta\mathscr F_f<0\)
from [Appendix D.5](#sec-d-5) to obtain the proposition.

When \(a_3<0\), the characteristic polynomial is negative at 0 and positive toward positive infinity, giving a positive real eigenvalue and an unstable equilibrium. A Hurwitz equilibrium necessarily has \(a_3>0\), so increasing \(\delta\) lowers local equilibrium fatigue under this section's conditions. This does not prove reachability from arbitrary initial states or classify every nonhyperbolic equilibrium. In general, \(a_3>0\) alone does not replace the other two Hurwitz inequalities.

On a regular branch, \(\mathscr N_\delta\ne0\), hence \(\Gamma_\delta\ne1\). At \(a_3=0\), the stated form of the implicit function theorem does not apply. A singular Jacobian alone does not establish an ordinary fold or saddle-node bifurcation. If the curve is parameterized by \(f\) as in the next section, an ordinary projection fold additionally requires \(\delta'(f)=0,\ \delta''(f)\ne0\). A dynamical bifurcation further requires a simple zero eigenvalue and the relevant nondegeneracy conditions. These do not follow from singularity alone.

This proposition excludes shared effort competition among multiple negative axes, nonzero task/recall effort, state-dependent sources, ongoing personality adaptation, and override policies. Under time rescaling, \(\mathscr N_\delta,a_3\) is divided by the cube of the scale factor, while \(df_*/d\delta\) and \(\Gamma_\delta\) are unchanged.

<a id="sec-d-9"></a>

### D.9 Equilibrium Curves, Uniqueness, and Conditional Guarantees for Defaults

Retain all conditions of [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8). Let
\[
\beta=\frac{a_f\omega}{b_fq}>0,\qquad
U_{\rm req}(f)=\frac{f}{\beta+(1-\beta)f},\qquad
f_{\rm cap}=\frac{\beta u}{1-u+\beta u}.
\]
\(U_{\rm req}\) is the effort required at equilibrium, not another dynamic controller. Active roots require \(0<U_{\rm req}<u\), equivalently \(0<f<f_{\rm cap}\). Emotion, background, and fatigue equilibrium sequentially give
\[
d(f)=
\frac{\lambda\alpha(A-b)}
{\lambda D_\alpha/(1-\kappa)+
\eta U_{\rm req}(f)/(1+\rho_ff)},\qquad
S(f)=d(f)-\theta^r,
\]
\[
\boxed{
\delta_{\rm eq}(f)=
(1+\rho_ff)^\chi S(f)
\left[\frac{u}{U_{\rm req}(f)}-1\right].
}
\]
Use these formulas only on
\[
\mathcal I_f=\{f:0<f<f_{\rm cap},\ S(f)>0\}
\]
For specified \(\delta_r>0\), every root satisfying
\(\delta_{\rm eq}(f)=\delta_r\) gives
\[
e_*=b+\frac{d(f)}{1-\kappa},\qquad
m_*=b+\frac{\kappa d(f)}{1-\kappa},\qquad
w_*=F(e_*,m_*).
\]
Conversely, every equilibrium on this branch has this representation. From
\(0<d(f)<\alpha(1-\kappa)(A-b)/D_\alpha\) and
\(D_\alpha\ge\alpha\),
we obtain \(b<m_*<e_*<A\le1\). The short-term spatial constraint is preserved by \(F\) and the readout formula. These roots are therefore not arbitrary out-of-domain algebraic solutions.

Differentiate
\(\mathcal V(z(f),\delta_{\rm eq}(f))=0\)
along this equilibrium curve, multiply on the left by the adjugate, and take the third component to obtain
\[
-a_3+\mathscr N_\delta\,\delta_{\rm eq}'(f)=0,\qquad
\boxed{\delta_{\rm eq}'(f)=a_3/\mathscr N_\delta.}
\]
This identity does not presuppose \(a_3\ne0\). At regular points,
\(df_*/d\delta_r=1/\delta_{\rm eq}'(f)\); negative slope corresponds to
\(a_3>0,\Gamma_\delta<1\), while positive slope corresponds to
\(a_3<0,\Gamma_\delta>1\) and instability.

If the curve is strictly decreasing over the entire admissible set, meaning that any two admissible \(f_1<f_2\) satisfy
\(\delta_{\rm eq}(f_1)>\delta_{\rm eq}(f_2)\), there is at most one root for the same \(\delta_r\). Decreasing separately on disconnected intervals does not guarantee uniqueness over their union. Strict decrease may also include zero-derivative points; \(\Gamma_\delta<1\) applies on that basis only at regular points. Negative slope generally does not replace the complete Hurwitz criterion.

**Sufficient condition \(\beta\ge1\): unique, locally stable active root.**

If
\[
a_f\omega\ge b_fq>0,\qquad
S(0):=\frac{\alpha(1-\kappa)(A-b)}{D_\alpha}-\theta^r>0,
\],
then for every finite \(\delta_r>0\) there is exactly one active equilibrium and its \(J_3\) is Hurwitz.

**Uniqueness proof.** Direct differentiation gives
\[
\frac d{df}\frac{U_{\rm req}(f)}{1+\rho_ff}
=
\frac{\beta-\rho_f(1-\beta)f^2}
{[\beta+(1-\beta)f]^2(1+\rho_ff)^2}>0.
\]
Thus \(d,S\) is strictly decreasing, and \(\mathcal I_f\) is one interval starting from 0. Let
\[
B_u(f)=\frac{u}{U_{\rm req}(f)}-1
=(1-u+u\beta)\frac{f_{\rm cap}-f}{f}>0.
\]
On \(\mathcal I_f\),
\[
\frac{\delta_{\rm eq}'}{\delta_{\rm eq}}
=\frac{\chi\rho_f}{1+\rho_ff}+\frac{S'}S
-\frac1f-\frac1{f_{\rm cap}-f}<0,
\],
because the first term is strictly less than \(1/f\) and \(S'<0\). Also,
\(\delta_{\rm eq}(0^+)=\infty\),
while at the right endpoint of the admissible interval, \(S\) or \(B_u\) tends to zero, hence \(\delta_{\rm eq}\to0\). Continuity and strict decrease give a unique root for every positive \(\delta_r\). If \(S(0)\le0\), there is no active root. Equality involves the threshold branch, whose nonlinear stability this proposition does not classify.

**Hurwitz proof.** Let the weighted rate be
\(\mathfrak a=a_f\omega,\ \mathfrak b=b_fq\), and let
\[
H_s=\frac{\eta dU_S}{s_f},\qquad
h=\frac{\eta U}{s_f}+H_s.
\]
At fatigue equilibrium,
\[
BU=\mathfrak b f,\qquad k_f=\frac{\mathfrak a\mathfrak b}{B},
\]
\[
\frac{B\rho_fU}{s_f}-k_f
=\frac{\mathfrak b[
\rho_f(\mathfrak b-\mathfrak a)f^2-\mathfrak a]}
{s_fB}<0
\]
(the last step uses \(\mathfrak a\ge\mathfrak b>0\)).
From \(gr=H_sB(\rho_fU/s_f-U_f)\),
\[
0\le gr\le\nu H_s\le\nu h.
\]
Write \(X=\lambda+h+\lambda_m\). Substitution into the coefficients of [Appendix D.8](#sec-d-8) gives
\[
a_2\ge\lambda_mL+\nu(\lambda+\lambda_m)>0,
\]
\[
a_3\ge\lambda_m\nu[
\lambda D_\alpha+(h-H_s)(1-\kappa)]>0.
\]
Furthermore, direct expansion using the same upper bound gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
a_1a_2-a_3
&=\lambda_mLX+\nu X(X+\nu)
-gr[\lambda+h+\nu+\lambda_m\kappa]\\
&\ge\lambda_mLX+
\nu[(\lambda+\lambda_m)(X+\nu)+h\lambda_m(1-\kappa)]>0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Every Hurwitz condition therefore holds. The equilibrium is locally exponentially stable within this smooth three-dimensional subsystem.

\(w\) is a stable cascade state, whose error satisfies
\[
|w(\tau)-w_*|
\le e^{-\lambda_x\tau}|w(0)-w_*|
+\lambda_x L_F\int_0^\tau e^{-\lambda_x(\tau-v)}
\|(e(v),m(v))-(e_*,m_*)\|\,dv,
\],
