where \(L_F<\infty\) is a Lipschitz constant of \(F\). Exponential decay of the three-dimensional error bounds the right-hand side by an exponential with a positive decay rate. Adding \(w,x\) therefore retains local exponential stability in the same reduced system with fixed external quantities. Even when the projection lies exactly on a box boundary, clip need not be assumed differentiable there.

**Implications for defaults.** The defaults
\(\beta=.02/.01=2\)
in [Section 12.3](#sec-12-3) satisfy this sufficient condition. When sources, background, and demand satisfy the remaining assumptions, uniqueness and local stability of the active equilibrium are analytical results. This is stronger than agreement across finitely many source settings, but is not a global theorem for the complete multi-axis feedback loop. It also does not guarantee stability of every coarse numerical step under default parameters.

\(\beta\ge1\) is a sufficient condition for this model, not a necessary condition, new global parameter restriction, or recommendation that greater fatigue is more human-like. It is invariant under consistent time-unit conversion. Boundedness, equilibrium uniqueness, and local stability do not replace human validity of fatigue and emotion. They do not justify arbitrarily amplifying fatigue or changing personality-learning speed.

<a id="sec-d-10"></a>

### D.10 Bistability with Nondefault Parameters

Use the same nondefault parameters as [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6) to analyze the invariant \((e,m,w,f)\) subsystem with other states and inputs fixed.

Let
\[
\theta=\frac{1282099}{5651000},\quad
U(f)=\frac{f}{.01+.99f},\quad
d(f)=\frac{1+199f+9900f^2}{4+2796f+39600f^2}.
\]
For \(0<f<1\), the denominator is strictly positive. The condition
\(\delta_{\rm eq}(f)=1/100000\) is equivalent to
\[
[d(f)-\theta](1-f)-f/1000=0.
\]
Clearing positive denominators yields the exact polynomial
\[
\begin{aligned}
N(f)
&=522604-2460745012f+7618179208f^2-5397559200f^3\\
&=(2f-1)(-522604+2459699804f-2698779600f^2).
\end{aligned}
\]
Hence the candidate roots are exactly
\[
f_-=\frac{2459699804-\sqrt{6044481553741324816}}{5397559200},
\qquad f_0=\frac12,
\]
\[
f_+=\frac{2459699804+\sqrt{6044481553741324816}}{5397559200}.
\]
The two outer roots have strict rational isolating intervals:
\[
\frac{2125161307972}{10^{16}}<f_-<
\frac{2125161307973}{10^{16}},
\]
\[
\frac{9111993694488921}{10^{16}}<f_+<
\frac{9111993694488924}{10^{16}}.
\]
The quadratic factor has opposite signs at each interval's endpoints, and the intervals are disjoint. A quadratic has at most two roots, so these are all outer roots. The displayed precision belongs to internal algebraic analysis, not high-precision values sent through the final eleven-axis output.

From the root equation,
\[
S=d-\theta=\frac{f}{1000(1-f)}>0,\qquad
e=2d,\quad m=d,\quad w=\frac73d.
\]
All three roots have \(0<d<1/4,\ e<A=1/2\), with
\(w\in(0,1)\) and \(x=e\) strictly inside the constraint box, making them admissible smooth equilibria. If regulation were inactive, equilibrium would require \(f=0,d=1/4>\theta\), which instead requires active regulation; there is no additional equilibrium on the inactive branch. The stated invariant subsystem therefore has exactly three equilibria.

**Stability of the two outer roots.** Substitute the isolating intervals into the rational coefficient formulas of [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6). Every denominator has a positive interval. Rational upper/lower-bound arithmetic gives these strict lower bounds:

| Equilibrium | Lower bound on \(a_1\) | Lower bound on \(a_2\) | Lower bound on \(a_3\) | Lower bound on \(a_1a_2-a_3\) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| \(f_-\) | \(213009\) | \(204719\) | \(510\) | \(43600000000\) |
| \(f_+\) | \(1126/1000\) | \(1509/100000\) | \(5182/100000000\) | \(1696/100000\) |

These are conservative exact positive bounds, not sign judgments from rounded eigenvalues. Every rational function and root interval used is specified here and in [Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6). Both outer roots therefore satisfy the Hurwitz conditions. Including \(w\) with eigenvalue \(-1/4\) gives two distinct locally exponentially attracting equilibria.

[Appendix D.6](#sec-d-6) already establishes \(a_3<0\) for the middle root. Its cubic has at least one positive real root. Since \(a_1>0\), the root sum is negative, and the product is \(-a_3>0\). The remaining roots are therefore both negative if real, or both have negative real parts if conjugate complex. This is a hyperbolic saddle with exactly one unstable direction; including \(w\) adds one stable direction.

**What follows and what does not.** Two attracting equilibria exist for the same fixed parameters and sources, establishing bistability with a nonempty local basin for each. Different complete initial states may yield different long-term outcomes. This is not a theorem that comparing only \(f(0)\) with 0.5 determines the outcome. The middle saddle is one point and cannot alone be a boundary in three- or four-dimensional state space. The global basin boundary has not been derived, nor has convergence of every initial state to one of the outer roots been proved.

For this parameter set \(\chi=0\), higher fatigue under fixed demand reduces effective regulation. Weaker regulation can increase deviation and demand, raising effort and fatigue load. This is positive feedback allowed by the equations; it establishes neither globally monotone trajectories nor an empirically verified human “fatigue trap.”

Bistability and initial-state dependence do not prove hysteresis under forward-and-reverse parameter changes. Hysteresis also involves the parameter path, branch continuation, and dynamic tracking. This specification makes no such claim and includes no corresponding scenarios or procedures. Two attractors under persistent sources do not contradict recovery after driving ceases under the conditions of Section 13 and Appendix B; the input conditions differ.

<a id="sec-d-11"></a>

### D.11 Equilibrium Criteria and Limits of Numerical Observation

**Mathematical result: conditions and model-specific derivation.** [Appendix D.9](#sec-d-9) proves that, under its remaining branch conditions, \(S(0)>0\), and \(\beta\ge1\), every finite positive \(\delta_r\) has a unique active root. Failure to observe a root on a finite grid therefore does not mean that the parameter set may have none. In particular, with other parameters fixed and \(\delta_r\to\infty\),
\[
U_{\rm req}(f)=f/\beta+O(f^2),\quad
C(f)=1+O(f),\quad S(f)=S(0)+O(f),
\]
so
\[
\delta_{\rm eq}(f)=\frac{u\beta S(0)}{f}+O(1)\qquad(f\downarrow0).
\]
Proved uniqueness, monotonicity, and endpoint limits imply that the unique root satisfies
\[
\boxed{\lim_{\delta_r\to\infty}\delta_r f_*(\delta_r)=u\beta S(0)>0.}
\]
The root may thus approach zero arbitrarily closely while remaining strictly positive for every finite positive parameter. This is an abstract parameter limit, not a calibration relation for human fatigue or a reason to change internal zeros.

Within the same restricted parameter class, multiple active roots exclude \(\beta\ge1\); conversely, \(\beta<1\) does not guarantee multiple roots. Sampled associations between multiplicity and parameter size establish no additional necessary conditions. “Extreme” is not a mathematical threshold without an explicit set and proof. Shared competition across axes, nonzero task/recall activity, ongoing learning, and state-dependent goals remain outside this single-axis guarantee.

Slope signs follow the exact relation in [Appendices D.8–D.9](#sec-d-8):
\[
\mathscr N_\delta<0,\qquad
\delta_{\rm eq}'=a_3/\mathscr N_\delta,\qquad
\operatorname{sgn}(\delta_{\rm eq}')=-\operatorname{sgn}(a_3).
\]
When \(a_3=0\), both sides are zero; at regular points, negative slope corresponds to \(a_3>0,\Gamma_\delta<1\).

Finite numerical observations do not replace these applicability conditions. Compare histories using identical valid inputs and conforming numerical approximations. Coarse steps, floating-point fixed points, and finite precision are handled in Appendix C.

<a id="sec-e"></a>

## Appendix E Data Compatibility and State Import

Assess import compatibility from the data's state semantics, fields, provenance, units, and representation contract. The rules below authorize neither reconstruction of complete internal states from outputs nor fabrication of missing historical data.

<a id="sec-e-1"></a>

### E.1 Retaining Admissible States and Accounting

For compatible data that can be restored directly, retain every admissible emotion state, numerical input, parameter, source and cause scope, event and its order, effort and goal override, deduplication and budget ledger, stored historical envelope, phase, definition version, and time contract. Also retain the supplied shared-time mapping. Do not replay history, reset admissible psychological states, clear accounting, or learn/rewrite memory-policy parameters because of import. Imports with missing fields or different experience-summary formats follow [Appendix E.5](#sec-e-5) and [Appendix E.6](#sec-e-6).

Identical admissible initial values, exact parameters, inputs, and event semantics define the same real-valued process under the stated uniqueness conditions. Adding provenance labels or analytical data does not change the trajectory for valid numerical inputs. Changing representations or arithmetic methods, however, does not guarantee identical finite-precision histories or long-term numerical values.

<a id="sec-e-2"></a>

### E.2 Historical Envelopes and Time Semantics

Normal new memories retain emotion/time envelopes, phase, source-state versions, and definition versions under Section 17. References may be added to imported existing entries only when their historical snapshots and times are traceable. Missing past emotion values or times are explicitly incomplete/unavailable: do not backfill from current states, replay history, or reallocate the original memory's emotion budget. Future shared fields are disabled by default and registered under [17.13](#sec-17-13). A complete emotion restoration file differs from an eleven-axis snapshot in memory; joint persistence follows [17.11–17.13](#sec-17-11) and [Appendix F.2](#sec-f-2).

\(\tau,T_{\rm ref}\) have the respective emotion-specific aliases \(\tau_E,T_E\). Aliases require no state reset or historical recomputation. Independent use requires no memory-clock parameters. Actual integration supplies the ratio, shared reference point, and interface contract under [17.6–17.14](#sec-17-6) and [Appendices F.1–F.3](#sec-f-1). Memory ages already stored with emotion-time semantics retain them and are not automatically relabeled in memory-local units. Unknown provenance or units must be explicitly marked missing, not guessed. Joint restoration data follow [17.11](#sec-17-11); output remains eleven-dimensional.

The information sets and decision semantics of [17.14](#sec-17-14) do not rewrite historical snapshots, increase their original precision, or create psychological events. If existing memory rules use thresholds, retain their then-known comparison objects, equality conventions, and numerical semantics. Mark unknowns as unknown; applying a new rule does not establish that the original classification used it. The bounds in [Appendix F.3](#sec-f-3) apply only under their dependency and regularity conditions and enable no new feedback.

<a id="sec-e-3"></a>

### E.3 Numerical Representation and Analytical Data

When numbers are stored as text, record the known original format and codec semantics. An explicit round-trip contract permits restoring the same finite value in that format. Display strings without this information retain unknown provenance and precision; they must not masquerade as restored original values. High-precision representation and analysis obey the same numerical semantics in [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5).

Store exact parameter sources separately from the representations actually used in evaluation, keeping unknowns marked unknown. To trace numerical results, retain original exact parameter sources if known, actual stored values, arithmetic/rounding semantics, and step-size representation. Do not fabricate original bits from decimal displays with unknown provenance or encoding. Interpret numerical histories using their actual rounding, steps, event alignment, and statistical definitions. Interpret numerical differences under [Appendix C.7](#sec-c-7), without resetting psychological history.

\(\epsilon^{\rm rep},\epsilon^{\rm ar},\epsilon^{\rm num}\) in [Appendix C.5](#sec-c-5), error bounds in [Appendix C.6](#sec-c-6), and readout semantics, representation-source decomposition, derived-quantity compatibility domains, poststorage bounds, remainder bounds, boundary derivatives, rounding cells, defects, and statistics are analytical definitions. They add no psychological state, memory update, event, or output axis, and do not reset baselines, rewrite history, or alter core equations or engineering initial values. Retaining a computational state or adding error-source classifications does not remove past errors; a nonconforming numerical implementation cannot claim that its errors thereby disappear.

The change of variables in [Appendix C.2](#sec-c-2) analyzes roots of the same frozen map; it does not replace it with another integrator. [Appendix C.1](#sec-c-1) gives conditional numerical-stability bounds, and [Appendices D.8–D.10](#sec-d-8) analyze feedback loops. None adds a psychological mechanism. Finite-step results must obey the numerical-approximation contract; differences from finer steps are not updates to psychological mechanisms.

Updating the quantization implementation does not write back to psychological state. Correcting floating-point rounding errors may change output grid points, but the exact-arithmetic function in [15.2](#sec-15-2) is unchanged.

<a id="sec-e-4"></a>

### E.4 Effort Provenance and Override Validity

Retain currently effective overrides and their expiry times under [9.3.4](#sec-9-3-4). An imported record without a validity interval must not be retained indefinitely; mark the interval unknown and make the override ineffective.

Validate effort-source enumeration and eligibility. An invalid label does not become valid because the original implementation ignored it. Reject it or perform an evidence-supported import repair under [9.3.4](#sec-9-3-4), without recomputing or clearing emotion states.

For compatible data missing provenance labels, mark automatic-policy output policy_generated and retain the original evidence classification of known external effort. Mark existing external values without provenance records legacy_unspecified, preserving what is unknown rather than fabricating observations or inferential evidence. legacy_unspecified is only for compatible imports, not a valid source for new live input. Full provenance eligibility follows [9.3.4](#sec-9-3-4).

<a id="sec-e-5"></a>

### E.5 Missing Fields and Complete-Restoration Conditions

When the source format is confirmed not to define \(\chi,q_{\rm rest},g_E\) and all remaining admissible complete internal states and accounting can be compatibly imported, use the stated engineering defaults for those fields and mark them accordingly, without fabricating historical observations. After import, use this specification's \(u_E\), compensation/recovery rules, and eleven-axis output. Do not replay history or reset \(f\) on that basis.

Ten-axis outputs alone cannot restore the original \(f\) or the complete system. Explicitly identify reinitialization; do not call it lossless import.

<a id="sec-e-6"></a>

### E.6 Importing Experience Summaries

When importing a different summary format, admissible psychological states and existing memory ledgers may be retained without retrospective event recomputation. An unknown complete experience profile must not be fabricated by filling unsaved axes with zero. The most conservative option is to explicitly discard unintegrated experience, set the summary to \(Q_E=P^E=0\), retain the already formed \(b\), and record the discarded amount and reason.

If a complete ten-axis profile is recorded, import its remaining fraction in the original semantic order using [10.3](#sec-10-3), explicitly accounting for capacity-related discards. Already integrated fractions must not be issued again. New counters may start at the import point, with the historical total ledger stored separately; they must not masquerade as the complete history under this specification's summary formulas.

<a id="sec-f"></a>

## Appendix F Conditional Guarantees for the Coupled Memory System

<a id="sec-f-1"></a>

### F.1 Limits on Independence, Stability, and Joint Error Claims

Separate existence, boundedness, or stability under some inputs for each theory does not establish all these properties after coupling. [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9) gives existence, uniqueness, and domain preservation under a particular connection contract. Attraction, long-term memory behavior, parameter identifiability, and human validity remain distinct claims.

The following directly analyzable conditional stability certificate is not assumed to hold automatically for the complete current emotion model. For two coupled trajectories with identical external inputs, shared event timing, and modes, use fixed coordinate scales and let \(d_E,d_M\ge0\) be dimensionless distances between the compared states, absolutely continuous between events and satisfying almost everywhere
\[
\dot d_E\le-\alpha_Ed_E+\beta_{EM}d_M,\qquad
\dot d_M\le\beta_{ME}d_E-\alpha_Md_M
\]
(derivatives with respect to \(\tau_c\)). Here
\(\alpha_E,\alpha_M>0\) and \(\beta_{EM},\beta_{ME}\ge0\) are finite constants. These distance-estimate coefficients must be derived from the coupled equations; they are neither renamed layer rates \(\lambda\) nor new psychological parameters. The distances must include every feedback state in the compared loop, without omitting variables that still affect either module.

If
\[
\alpha_E\alpha_M>\beta_{EM}\beta_{ME},
\],
choose
\[
\frac{\beta_{EM}}{\alpha_M}<w<
\frac{\alpha_E}{\beta_{ME}},
\]
Interpret the upper endpoint as \(+\infty\) when \(\beta_{ME}=0\), with \(w>0\). Let
\[
V_w=d_E+wd_M,\qquad
\rho=\min\{\alpha_E-w\beta_{ME},
\alpha_M-\beta_{EM}/w\}>0.
\]
Adding the two distance inequalities yields
\[
\dot V_w\le-\rho V_w.
\]
If shared-batch jumps also satisfy
\(V_w(\tau_{c,k}^+)\le g_kV_w(\tau_{c,k}^-)\),
\(0\le g_k<\infty\), then
\[
V_w(\tau_c)\le
e^{-\rho(\tau_c-\tau_{c,0})}
\prod_{\tau_{c,0}<\tau_{c,k}\le\tau_c}g_k\,
V_w(\tau_{c,0}).
\]
This follows by integration between events and concatenation across finitely many jumps. Having all \(g_k\le1\) is one sufficient contraction condition; more generally, the propagation factor on the right must tend to zero in the long term. This certificate is unavailable without the distance bounds, when quantized jumps cross boundaries outside the bound's scope, or when trajectories have different event timing. Failure of these sufficient conditions does not itself prove instability.

Converting distance rates from memory-local time to shared \(\tau_c\) multiplies every coefficient in the second row by \(r_T\); converting only \(\alpha_M\) while omitting the cross coefficient is invalid. The certificate concerns trajectory differences under the same input. It requires neither convergence of all histories and inputs to one equilibrium nor human validity.

**Exact condition for a fixed two-dimensional comparison system and scaling limits.** Temporarily write
\(a=\alpha_E>0,\ d=\alpha_M>0,\ b=\beta_{EM}\ge0,\ c=\beta_{ME}\ge0\),
where \(b\) is shorthand for this proof, not the personality-baseline vector. The constant-coefficient comparison matrix is
\[
A=\begin{pmatrix}-a&b\\c&-d\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\det(\lambda I-A)=\lambda^2+(a+d)\lambda+(ad-bc).
\]
It has nonnegative off-diagonal entries (Metzler); its eigenvalues are real, with largest eigenvalue
\[
s(A)=-\frac{a+d}{2}
+\frac{\sqrt{(a-d)^2+4bc}}2.
\]
Since \(a+d>0\),
\[
A\text{ is Hurwitz}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad ad>bc
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\exists w>0:\ a-cw>0,\ d-b/w>0.
\]
Existence of a suitable weight is therefore an exact condition for this two-dimensional constant-coefficient comparison system. It is not a necessary-and-sufficient reduction of stability for the actual nonlinear, switching, or quantized hybrid system. Establishing comparison inequalities, choosing state distances, and handling jumps may still be conservative.

If \(b,c>0\) and \(ad>bc\), the two rate terms respectively decrease and increase with \(w\). Their intersection
\[
w_*=\frac{a-d+\sqrt{(a-d)^2+4bc}}{2c}
\]
maximizes
\(\rho(w)=\min\{a-cw,d-b/w\}\), with
\[
\rho(w_*)=\frac{a+d-\sqrt{(a-d)^2+4bc}}2=-s(A)>0.
\]
If a cross coefficient is zero, use the original weight interval. The optimal rate may have only a supremum as \(w\to0\) or \(w\to\infty\); the divided formulas above then do not apply. If both are zero, any \(w>0\) gives \(\rho=\min(a,d)\). Feasibility need not rely on blind weight search, but feasible weights may give different rate bounds and alter jump gains \(g_k\). No extra conservatism in feasibility does not make weights irrelevant to every analysis.

Now let \(r>0\) scale only the second row, with the other base estimates fixed:
\[
A(r)=\begin{pmatrix}-a&b\\rc&-rd\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\det A(r)=r(ad-bc),\quad \operatorname{tr}A(r)=-a-rd.
\]
Hurwitz stability / feasibility is therefore independent of \(r\), but the feasible weight interval is
\[
\frac{b}{rd}<w_r<\frac{a}{rc}.
\]
If \(w_1\) is feasible, choosing \(w_r=w_1/r\) gives
\[
\rho_r=\min\{a-cw_1,\ r(d-b/w_1)\}>0.
\]
The upper endpoint is still infinity when \(c=0\). A fixed old weight need not remain feasible for every \(r\). One may take \(r=r_T\) here only if the operation is exactly this row scaling, with other estimates and the analyzed domain unchanged. Pure time-unit conversion under [Section 14](#sec-14) and [Sections 17.6–17.7](#sec-17-6) transforms every quantity consistently and represents the same process. Changing physical memory rates or coupling itself may change trajectories, operating domains, coefficient estimates, and event generation. Capture/storage jumps are also outside this determinant criterion. It does not show that the complete emotion–memory system's stability is always unaffected by relative timescales.

For joint numerical error, replace \(Z\) in [Appendix C.7](#sec-c-7) with the complete joint state, provided the shared-time vector field, events, reconstruction, and Lipschitz bounds satisfy all assumptions there. Different numerical step sizes in the modules may merely partition their respective solves, but both must solve for the same effective times and mathematical input functions. Holding old outputs, interpolating, missing boundaries, or changing event order must be explicit interface definitions or approximation errors. Sharing \(t\) alone does not eliminate coupling error.

<a id="sec-f-2"></a>

### F.2 Mathematical Guarantees and Unresolved Aspects of Encapsulation

Fix admissible initial emotion states and inputs, recorded content and identifiers, effective recording times, phase, versions, and the order in [Section 17.8](#sec-17-8). A unique emotion trajectory makes \(Q_{\rm out}(Z_E^{\rm read})\) single-valued. The time mapping is also single-valued, so snapshots and their envelopes are unique. If memory's envelope-commit map is likewise single-valued, domain-preserving, and atomically deduplicated, it can be concatenated in order with emotion events under [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9), retaining a unique joint trajectory. Quantization has specified boundary decisions; discontinuity does not automatically destroy uniqueness under fixed timing.

Capture is a pure read, and memory-envelope commitment projects to the identity on \(Z_E\). They therefore do not themselves violate emotion domains, long-term spatial constraints, the fatigue domain, or the personality-anchoring domain. Retransmitting the same effect_id and payload is also the identity map. This does not mean that later valid recall triggered by new memories has no emotional effect; distinguish those actions by their own event IDs.

Envelopes may contain emotional history, so the coupled system has data dependence in both directions and cannot be called unidirectional. If there is no instantaneous emotion feedthrough within an interval and all historical data belong to memory's committed state, the continuous vector field and bound in [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9) apply under those conditions. All capture/storage jumps are separately included in the event stream.

Existence and uniqueness do not imply Lipschitz sensitivity or contraction. Near a quantization boundary, arbitrarily small internal emotion differences may produce historical readings one grid spacing apart. A finite global Lipschitz gain therefore cannot be assigned directly to the capture map. Applying [Appendix C.7](#sec-c-7) or [Appendix F.1](#sec-f-1) requires valid jump-error/gain bounds for the state region, explicit boundary conditions, or suitable alternative analysis. Capture must not automatically be treated as \(g_k=1\). Adding historical entries also does not imply contraction of a distance containing the complete archive.

If memory later uses emotional context for formation, retention, or retrieval, or enables the live shared state of [Section 17.13](#sec-17-13), include those dependencies in the complete coupled equations and proofs. Bounds omitting them do not apply automatically. If their effects are already fully represented in \(Z_M,F_M,H_M\) and valid estimates, their emotional provenance does not invalidate [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9); distinguish the cases under [Appendix F.3](#sec-f-3). This specification defines the encapsulation and definition-source contracts. Unavailable internal memory theory, psychological coefficients, and specific feedback functions remain unspecified; assuming their correctness does not replace an integration proof.

<a id="sec-f-3"></a>

### F.3 Distinguishing Historical Dependence, Instantaneous Feedback, and Joint Conditions

Analyze dependence on historical data separately from instantaneous feedback from current emotion. Applicability depends on actual states, interval flows, jumps, and event-generation rules; estimates must cover every relevant dependency.

| Dependency form | Location in this specification | Applicability of the conditions |
|---|---|---|
| Memory's own use of committed historical values | Historical data belong to \(Z_M\); effects enter \(F_M,H_M\) or memory's own events | If the bounds already cover these states and functions, the interval-flow form and \(\Lambda_c\) of [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9) still apply. After adding an actual function, do not reuse numerical bounds that omit it |
| Writing emotion snapshots into memory | Capture/envelope jumps in [Section 17.12](#sec-17-12) | Interval flows may retain their form. Jumps must be single-valued, domain-preserving, and locally finite; sensitivity is a separate question |
| Holding the latest committed value as input | Optional buffer and commit jumps in [Section 17.10](#sec-17-10) | The buffer belongs to the complete state and is held within intervals. Analyze flows and jumps separately; this is not instantaneous current-emotion feedthrough |
| Continuously reading current emotion | Additional dependence of \(U_M\) on \(Z_E\) | Bounds omitting this path do not apply directly. Compose the added terms and their regularity bounds |
| A genuinely shared live state | \(S\) and its single evolution in [Section 17.13](#sec-17-13) | Use the actual joint domain and equations for \(S,Z_E^{\rm private},Z_M^{\rm private}\); do not treat duplicate copies of \(S\) as independent coordinates |

**Fixed history is distinct from discontinuity in time.** Let \(h_\nu=y_\nu^{\rm hist}\) be an original historical snapshot that remains available. On an interval where it is not replaced, removed, or switched out of selection,
\[
\frac{dh_\nu}{d\tau_c}=0.
\]
Using this fixed quantity in a continuously evolving function does not make it discontinuous in time within that interval merely because its creation involved quantization. If the selected record or held value changes, the change occurs at a separately defined switch/commit point. With local finiteness in shared time and interval vector fields satisfying [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9), the existence-and-uniqueness argument still supports concatenation. State-derived switching rules must additionally satisfy that section's deterministic-generation and non-Zeno conditions; not every threshold selector is automatically compliant.

This differs from asking whether a small change in initial state produces another capture grid point. The former concerns evolution in time with fixed history; the latter concerns state sensitivity across trajectories under [Section 17.14](#sec-17-14). Historical data represented as discrete-mode data need not be treated as differentiable continuous coordinates. If included in a continuous-state distance, the required bounds must actually hold. When two trajectories permanently retain different histories, their complete-state distance including those differences need not tend to zero. Recovery of other components does not establish the complete-state contraction of [Appendix F.1](#sec-f-1).

**Bounds when a current-emotion pathway is added.** As a conditional form for a future interface only, suppose a fixed-mode interval explicitly specifies
\[
U_M=\mathcal A_{EM}(\tau_c,Z_E,Z_M,U^{\rm ext}),
\],
while \(U_E\) remains determined by the actual complete adapter, with every dependency explicit and no unresolved algebraic loop. Under identical external inputs, if nonnegative local bounds satisfy
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\Delta U_E\|&\le A_E\|\Delta Z_E\|+A_M\|\Delta Z_M\|,\\
\|\Delta U_M\|&\le B_E\|\Delta Z_E\|+B_M\|\Delta Z_M\|,\\
\|\Delta F_E\|&\le L_{Ez}\|\Delta Z_E\|+L_{Eu}\|\Delta U_E\|,\\
\|\Delta F_M\|&\le L_{Mz}\|\Delta Z_M\|+L_{Mu}\|\Delta U_M\|,
\end{aligned}
\],
then for the shared-time vector field
\(\mathcal V_c=(F_E,r_TF_M)\) and the sum norm,
\[
\|\Delta\mathcal V_c\|
\le c_E^{\rm Lip}\|\Delta Z_E\|
+c_M^{\rm Lip}\|\Delta Z_M\|,
\]
\[
c_E^{\rm Lip}=L_{Ez}+L_{Eu}A_E+r_TL_{Mu}B_E,\qquad
c_M^{\rm Lip}=L_{Eu}A_M+r_T(L_{Mz}+L_{Mu}B_M).
\]
One may therefore use the tighter bound
\[
\Lambda_c^{\rm fb}=\max\{c_E^{\rm Lip},c_M^{\rm Lip}\},
\],
or the bound more directly comparable to [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9):
\[
\boxed{
\overline\Lambda_c^{\rm fb}
=L_{Ez}+L_{Eu}(A_E+A_M)
+r_T\bigl[L_{Mz}+L_{Mu}(B_E+B_M)\bigr].
}
\]
The derivation substitutes the two input-difference bounds into their vector fields and adds them. The largest column coefficient bounds the sum norm, and with nonnegative coefficients the maximum is no greater than their sum. When \(B_E=B_M=0\), the latter expression recovers [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9). These constants are analytical bounds, not psychological parameters; \(c_E^{\rm Lip}\) is also distinct from the engineering timescale-separation coefficient \(c_E\).

The joint existence-and-uniqueness argument applies only if these bounds hold on the analyzed region, the composed bound and local vector-field bounds are time-integrable, and event, admissible-input-domain, continuation, and non-Zeno conditions remain satisfied. The uniformly bounded adapter conditions of [Section 17.9](#sec-17-9) may be used. Separate integrability of factors does not prove integrability of their product. The original \(A_E,A_M,L_{Mz}\) must also hold for the actual new dependencies; merely appending new-path coefficients to old estimates is insufficient.

If \(\mathcal A_{EM}\) directly contains current \(Q_{\rm out}(Z_E)\), [Section 17.14](#sec-17-14) shows that finite \(B_E\) is generally not guaranteed. Subsequent functions may eliminate some jump differences, however, so the complete composition must still be analyzed. The converse claim that every such equation lacks a solution or uniqueness is also invalid. Supply an appropriate solution concept and proof for the vector field, or use an explicitly defined locally finite commit interface. Do not omit discontinuous terms and then cite this bound. The analysis enables no new control pathway, exposes no additional internal emotion axes, and leaves the eleven-axis interface unchanged.

**Event and responsibility conditions.** Whenever memory formation, persistence, retrieval, or new recall generates events, their triggers, shared time, occurrence_id, effect type, and order must enter the joint event stream of [Section 17.8](#sec-17-8). Recording acknowledgements do not independently trigger new psychological events. Genuinely new retrieval must still obey evidence, budget, and personality-learning eligibility rules. If a live shared state is enabled, the joint admissible domain must also represent its compatibility constraints with both modules. An independent product domain cannot be reused while ignoring constraints on the same \(S\). These are mathematical conditions for valid integration, not claims of completed memory dynamics or human validity.
